Repubblica - Inter, già possibile un addio clamoroso. E Baccin vuole soffiare a Roma e Lazio...

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Il quotidiano La Repubblica parla dell'Inter e del mercato che verrà. A gennaio, quando la società nerazzurra potrebbe cercare un centrale sul mercato. "Ma la possibilità di intervenire sul mercato subito dipende dalle uscite", scrive il quotidiano.

"Il piano più probabile resta quello di rinviare l’investimento all’estate: per anticiparlo, la società spera di trovare già nella finestra invernale una soluzione per Yann Bisseck. Il tedesco ha rinnovato a luglio fino al 2031 e partirà soltanto di fronte a un’offerta adeguata. Una sua cessione permetterebbe ai nerazzurri di ricavare risorse da reinvestire nello stesso reparto e di aggiungere subito un’opzione alla difesa di Cristian Chivu. A giugno, poi, il quadro potrebbe cambiare ancora", si legge ancora.

Secondo lo stesso quotidiano anche Stones potrebbe lasciare il club nerazzurro a fine stagione, anche se ha un contratto fino al 2028. L'inglese è stato fermo per settimane per infortunio: "La continuità che riuscirà a trovare dopo il rientro peserà sulle valutazioni del club", si legge su La Repubblica.

Per l'estate si segue Oliver Provstgaard, centrale danese classe 2003 della Lazio di Gattuso. La società di Lotito aprirebbe ad una cessione a gennaio per 40 mln e per questo i due club potrebbero rinviare i discorsi a fine stagione. L'Inter tra l'altro sarebbe alla finestra per Mario Hermoso.

L'Inter sarebbe inoltre interessata ad Hermoso che ha un contratto con la Roma fino al 2027 e guadagna 3.5 mln all'anno più bonus. Il club giallorosso lavora al rinnovo fino al 2029 con opzione fino al 2030. Il club nerazzurro guarda dalla giusta distanza la trattativa tra il giocatore e la società per un calciatore che se non rinnovasse sarebbe sul mercato a parametro zero.

What’s next for Nvidia? Whatever doesn’t rock the mind-bogglingly profitable boat

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I want you to understand just how much money this company rakes in.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

I want you to understand just how much money this company rakes in.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If someone handed you $8,300 every second, what would you do? That’s how much pure profit Nvidia now makes. By the time you finish reading this paragraph, Nvidia will likely have cleared $45,000 and change.

It’s easy to look at Nvidia, a company that just lost its visionary founder CEO due to a series of scandals, and think that the company and maybe the entire AI industry are in terrible trouble. Perhaps so?

But I want you to understand: Nvidia isn’t your average semi-successful company that might tempt a new CEO to fix things that are broken. It is a company so staggeringly, mind-bogglingly successful it’ll do everything in its power not to rock the boat. There is so much money at stake.

It’s so much money that when I heard about Jensen Huang’s resignation, my first thought was: I need a way to visualize this jaw-dropping amount of money for you. I want to show you that Nvidia has risen so far, so fast, that rival chipmakers AMD, Intel, and Qualcomm barely qualify as competition now.

If you’re on a desktop OS, you could mouse over each dot in our chart above to see the staggering sums. But we built another way to visualize the rise of Nvidia I like even better. It’s a horizontal bar graph that not only puts Nvidia’s revenue growth in perspective, but shows you its monster profits right alongside them — plus the world events that made Nvidia a star.

TL;DR: The crypto boom and the great GPU shortage have nothing on the AI boom. Those earlier periods saw Nvidia’s profits briefly peak at $1.24 billion and $3 billion a quarter respectively, while the rise of data centers has driven them to $65 billion per quarter with no sign of stopping.

Yes, Jensen may have set a trillion dollars of Nvidia’s market cap on fire — from around $5 trillion at the start of November to “only” $3.6 trillion now — but the smoldering company is raking in $700 million in profit a day on average.

Put another way: Nvidia could give a $1 million bonus to every one of its 42,000 employees, every three months, without running out of cash. Or, you know, Nvidia could end world hunger or provide clean water for everyone, now that it’s making over $200 billion in pure profit a year.

Maybe that’d make people feel differently about data centers?

But even if we stick to self-serving capitalism, you’re probably still a happy investor if you bought Nvidia a decade ago. A $1,000 investment 10 years ago is now worth over $67,000. You’d have over $1 million if you invested $15,000 back then.

And make no mistake, Nvidia is sticking to capitalism. Nvidia interim CEO Colette Kress just promised to return as much money to investors as possible on the company’s November 17th earnings call. “Going forward, we intend to return all excess free cash flow net of strategic uses to shareholders,” she announced. “All” is a lot more than the 50 percent baseline that Jensen promised five months ago.

Nvidia isn’t the only company whose fortunes have risen this dramatically. RAMageddon revealed that just three companies — Samsung, SK Hynix, and Micron — controlled nearly 90 percent of the entire global DRAM market, and those three have repeatedly signaled that they’d prefer to celebrate record profits instead of ramping up production too fast.

But while their revenues and profits have been skyrocketing even faster over the past year or so, even Big RAM hasn’t caught the AI wave quite the same way as Nvidia. Samsung, of course, doesn’t just make memory — its revenue numbers include phones, tablets, TVs, home appliances, chips, displays, camera sensors, audio, networking equipment, medical equipment, and more.

In the most recent quarter, Micron made $37.7 billion in profit, SK Hynix made $29.8 billion, and Samsung came in at around $55.3 billion, but they’re all shy of Nvidia’s $65.3 billion. Historically, RAM has also been a bit more cyclical: Both Micron and SK Hynix actually lost money in 2023 because they produced too much memory.

On its latest earnings call, the first since cofounder Jensen Huang’s resignation, interim CEO and CFO Colette Kress reinforced the idea that Nvidia expects the good times to keep on rolling.

Asked how things might change under new management, she suggested that they wouldn’t: “From a business perspective, it’s business as usual,” she said. She added that the company has secured the backing of customers and suppliers including TSMC, the all-important firm that actually fabricates Nvidia’s chips.

“Our momentum is real, our demand is accelerating, our supply is growing. Our customers and partners are with us, and employees are committed,” she claimed. “We’ve got this.”

Nvidia claims it’ll rake in even more money next quarter, with projected revenue of $130 billion. Profit may be proportionally smaller since gross margins are estimated to drop to 71–72 percent (compared to nearly 75 percent), but Kress said Nvidia intends to get them up as high as 73 percent next year.

In other words: This astounding gravy train isn’t going anywhere. At least that’s the plan.

Stevie Bonifield contributed to this story.

Spend management platform Payhawk announces $280M raise, 2B valuation

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

UK-based fintech Payhawk announced a $280 million Series C funding round Wednesday, led by Goldman Sachs’ growth equity arm, making it one of Europe’s largest Series C rounds this year, as noted by Sifted. The round values the company at $2 billion, bringing its total funding to more than $500 million to date.

Founded in 2018, Payhawk is an enterprise financial management platform that helps companies track and manage expenses and procurement using AI agents. Speaking to FinTech magazine, Payhawk’s co-founder and CEO, Hristo Borisov, said the company saw “an incredible growth opportunity and room to bring finance teams AI-native products that combine agents, payment infrastructure, and enterprise controls.”

Payhawk competes with spend management companies Pleo, Moss, and Spendesk, and says it operates in 33 countries. Notably, the company said it still has 70% of the $100 million it raised in 2022, a round that valued the company a $1 billion. This fresh capital will support its global expansion. Other investors in the round include Greenoaks, Lightspeed, and QED.

GTA 6's Completely Unrealistic Mountains Had to Be in the Game, Rockstar Says

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GTA 6 takes some liberties with its world around Vice City

If you’ve ever spent time in and around Miami, Grand Theft Auto 6 is bound to delight you. Rockstar Games spared no expense in bringing the diverse world of Florida to life in digital form. Walk down the street in Vice City, and you’re sure to find an approximation of a bar you’ve actually been to. Head to the Leonida Keys, and you’ll find rows of houses on stilts. The attention to detail is impressive, but it also makes one odd detail about the map a little odd.

Go all the way north in Leonida, right up to Lummox County, and you’ll be able to go hiking and hunting in the mountains. As any Floridian will tell you, that’s not exactly reflective of the real Florida’s topography. Why take such a major creative liberty there when so much else about Leonida is grounded in realism? In an interview with Polygon, Rockstar North co-head Rob Nelson explained why GTA 6 takes players to the mountains.

Nelson said that one of the biggest challenges the team faced when designing the game was figuring out the map. Rockstar’s map team began working on the game in 2015, Nelson said, well before anyone else at the studio. While creating another city was easy enough for a team that has mastered that art, expanding to surrounding Florida locales was a tougher proposition. That’s because Florida is a very flat state.

The problem with depicting it as is, though, is that GTA players likely would have some expectations about what should be in a modern GTA map. GTA 5, for instance, built off of Los Angeles to create Los Santos. Because of that, the map has a lot of variety in elevation. That wouldn’t be possible if Leonida stuck too close to reality, so Rockstar ultimately decided to include some mountains to keep GTA 6 a little more consistent with GTA 5.

Though it may not be accurate, it’s a welcome addition to the map. As fun as it is to drive around Vice City, weaving around the same highways can get old. Lummox County offers a lot more open space for off-roading, biking, and other activities that just wouldn’t be possible in a city. For instance, when I found a bicycle perched atop a hill, I found myself racing down a natural BMX course that was built into the world. Every time I head up to Lummox County, it feels like I’m on vacation.

Crucially, it’s also the best date spot in the entire game. You can take Jason and Lucia out on impromptu dates throughout GTA 6, letting the couple play some mini-golf together or blow off steam at a shooting range. One of my favorite play sessions so far simply happened when I got the two in a truck and took them up north to a part of the map I’d never seen. We got out when we found a hiking trail and wound up stumbling into some ruins high up on a hill. It was a peaceful moment of discovery that the two got to share together. Moments like that are what make GTA 6 special, and Leonida’s intricate design is the thing that powers it all.

Rockstar’s magnum opus searches for love and catharsis in desperate times — and finds them

Miami artists’ Art Basel boycott campaign calls for alternatives to fair week’s commercial model

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Visitors to the 2025 edition of Art Basel Miami Beach interact with a sculpture by the collective MSCHF on the Perrotin stand Photo: Liliana Mora

As Art Basel Miami Beach prepares to return to the city, a group of local artists and organisers is asking the Miami art community to reconsider its relationship with the busy week of fairs that takes over the city every December. The grassroots collective Artists 4 Artists Miami announced plans for its “Art Basel Boycott” at a protest outside the Miami Beach Convention Center at the end of last year’s edition of the Switzerland-based fair. Now, after a year of preparation, the collective is launching its first campaign, inviting local artists, curators, gallerists, collectors and residents to reconsider their participation in Miami Art Week’s fairs while building alternatives rooted in the community.

In a city whose international cultural profile has become virtually inseparable from the Art Basel brand, the boycott poses a difficult question: can Miami’s arts economy sustain its local creative community without depending so heavily on the machinery of a global art fair? Oorganisers are not calling for a blanket rejection of Miami Art Week or even its tentpole event. Instead, organisers envision a longer-term effort to challenge the economic and political structures surrounding the fair and redirect attention toward local artists, independent galleries and experimental programming.

“We realise that in order to refuse to take part in something as intertwined and culturally impactful as Art Basel, you really have to start building an alternative,” says misael soto, an artist and founder of Artists 4 Artists Miami.

The campaign grew out of the collective’s broader efforts to connect cultural work with other grassroots struggles. After spending much of 2025 supporting campaigns led by other organisations, Artists 4 Artists Miami began considering how it could mobilise its own community. Its participation in protests organised by Jewish Voice for Peace and other groups opposing the war in Gaza helped crystallise the idea, soto says, prompting organisers to examine the connections between the art market, housing affordability, labour conditions, and the companies that benefit from the fair.

For soto, Art Basel has become a focal point for these overlapping concerns. The fair generates significant economic activity, but the group questions how much of that value reaches Miami’s working artists and residents, particularly as the city becomes increasingly expensive. The group also points to the environmental and infrastructural costs of hosting an international event, and to the political and corporate interests associated with some of its major partners and investors.

Those concerns extend to the fair’s relationship with the city itself; soto points to a recent Miami Beach Commission decision concerning Art Basel’s rental agreement for the Miami Beach Convention Center, which reduced the fair’s rent from nearly $1m to around $500,000 and committed the equivalent of an additional $300,000 in public funds toward first-responder and security costs. The group questions whether the benefits the fair brings to the community justify the public resources devoted to it.

And yet, as the Artists 4 Artists Miami organisers acknowledge, Art Basel is deeply embedded in Miami’s cultural identity and economy. For many artists and arts workers, Miami Art Week provides essential income through art handling, installation, production and other contract jobs, while offering a smaller number opportunities to exhibit their work. Local galleries rely on the annual influx of collectors, press and visitors, and cultural institutions benefit from the attention and potential donor relationships the week brings.

“Art Basel Miami Beach has been a committed partner to Miami Beach and its creative community for more than two decades, supporting local artists and organisations through cultural programming, educational initiatives and opportunities for artistic exchange,” a spokesperson for Art Basel tells The Art Newspaper. “The fair also generates significant economic activity across the region, benefiting local businesses and workers in a wide range of sectors.”

According to local officials, the 2025 edition of Art Basel Miami Beach generated nearly $60m in economic impact for the city of Miami Beach, and $570m in economic impact for Miami-Dade County. The fair has consistently included several South Florida galleries as exhibitors, and every year the city of Miami Beach acquires a work from the fair for its public art collection.

Ariel Lynch, an artist and educator who has lived in the city for four years, signed the Artists 4 Artists Miami pledge despite recognising the tensions inherent in the campaign. Lynch says the fair offers substantial economic benefits to established galleries and collectors while providing comparatively few exhibition opportunities for local artists.

“Miami deserves more than institutions like Art Basel Miami Beach,” Lynch says. She plans to spend Miami Art Week visiting local galleries, events and experimental exhibitions instead of attending the main fair, while introducing students to the city’s year-round art scene.

Still, Lynch acknowledges the difficult position facing those who depend on Art Basel for their livelihoods. For workers already struggling to make ends meet, declining paid work during Miami Art Week may not be financially feasible. That is a dilemma soto is also mindful of: the fairs have helped pay their bills and enabled them to travel internationally, but they believe the short-term benefits can obscure longer-term costs, including overwork, underappreciated labour and environmental strain.

The “Art Basel Boycott” pledge is designed to accommodate different levels of participation. Signatories must select at least one commitment: not attending Art Basel Miami Beach, not exhibiting work at the fair, not exhibiting during Miami Art Week or withholding their labour from the fair. They can also choose commitments aimed at supporting alternatives, allowing participants to assess what is realistic given their circumstances.

As of this writing, the campaign had collected 21 signatures through an online form hosted on the collective’s website. Organisers are contacting local cultural and political organisations, particularly grassroots groups, to seek endorsements and establish partnerships. One existing partner is Panamía, a local network connecting businesses, creatives and residents. Artists 4 Artists Miami is also exploring programming in conjunction with a Panamía gathering and art print fair planned for Miami Art Week.

Beyond events, the collective hopes to establish a mutual-support network that could help pledge signatories navigate the financial consequences of opting out of Miami Art Week. That would entail connecting artists and organisations with local resources, soto says, including food assistance and other forms of mutual aid.

For the boycott’s organisers and signatories, success will not necessarily be measured by a signature count or an immediate impact on the fair. Instead, they hope the campaign will encourage a gradual shift in how Miami’s cultural producers and workers understand their economic and political influence.

“If a year from now we see that much more alternative and locally focused programming happening outside of Art Week,” soto says, “that would be a huge victory.”

The initiative encourages property owners to lend their vacant spaces for free and offers $2,500 stipends to artists—but they may still need to pay rent

The fair will feature 272 exhibitors across a smaller footprint, with blue-chip galleries dispersed throughout the stands

The public programme includes a sculpture park and videos screened on Frank Gehry’s New World Center

Do commercial galleries stand to benefit from the increased footfall brought by the famous art fair?

GTA 6 changes the way you steal cars: here's everything you need to know about slim jims, key cloners, and tracking devices

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Cars have gotten smarter, but so have you.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Used to be that all you needed to steal a car in a GTA game was a few seconds and a sharp elbow. In GTA 6, you can still smash a window and hotwire some cars, but the car stealing system has changed a lot and the further you get into the game, the more tools you'll need.

That means a quick getaway in a stolen vehicle isn't always that simple. Here are the methods for stealing cars, the new tools you'll get and when they become available in the story, and everything else you need to know about boosting rides in GTA 6.

Want to steal a car? Walk up to the door and press the interact button. That'll tell you if it's locked (most are), if it's alarmed (nearly all are), and if it's keyless—a new type of car that we'll cover more below.

If you smash the window, you'll then climb into the car and need to hotwire it, which just takes a second or two and uses the control sticks to find the "sweet spot." If it's a keyless car, you won't be able to hotwire it or drive it away—again, more on that below. If you smash a window on an alarmed car, the alarm will sound, which can attract attention.

You can still pull people out of their cars, but be warned: many of them will fight back, meaning you'll need to hammer a button quickly to defeat them. Lose, and they'll clonk your head against the side of the car, knocking you down and speeding away. And even if you do pull them out of their car, this is Leonida: a lot of motorists are armed and aren't shy about opening fire.

You can't slim jim a car someone is already sitting in, so you'll have to break the window if the door is locked. Plus, there are car trackers to worry about now: more on that below.

In Chapter 1, in the sixth story mission called Unwanted Attention, your buddy Cal will help you install the "Waink It" app on your phone, which lets you scan cars to see their make and model, if they're locked, alarmed, keyless, or have trackers. The app will also tell you the condition of the car, how much it can be sold for, and what it costs to register them—registering stolen cars at Pay 'n' Spray shops is how you take ownership of them in GTA 6.

Want to get into a locked car without breaking the window or setting off the alarm? You'll need a slim jim, a tool that lets you pop the lock and open the door quietly.

You'll get this new tool in Chapter 2 of the main story, in the fifth mission called "Moses," where you meet an auto mechanic (Moses!) who will give you a slim jim and teach you how to use it. Now when you sidle up to a car door, you'll be able to use the slim jim to pop the lock: no broken windows, no alarms—though it takes a little longer than using your elbow. Again, you still won't be able to start keyless cars, even if you have a slim jim.

Here's the big one. Keyless cars can't be hotwired, so you'll need a key cloner. You'll get the key cloner in Chapter 3, in the 16th mission called "Steal the Zorruso for Randy." That's a long time to wait, but now you can stand next to the door of a keyless car and use your phone to clone its key.

Bonus: the key cloner will not only unlock the door but automatically start the car. That makes it worth the wait! However, keyless cars often come with another new system: trackers.

Not a big deal, really: if a car has a standard tracker, it just means you can't modify it at a mod shop unless you've paid to register the car as your own. I probably wouldn't modify a car unless I owned it anyway, so I never worried about standard trackers at all.

These can be a problem. Advanced trackers will notify the police that the car has been stolen and pinpoint your location—and the cops will respond quickly. To defeat the advanced tracker, you need to drive fast enough that the tracker won't be able to send a signal to the cops. Slow down, and it'll start beeping and you'll only have a few seconds to speed up again. If you fail to speed up enough, the tracker will pinpoint your location and the cops will be on you again.

You have to drive pretty fast to keep the advanced tracker from triggering, which makes it tougher to get them to a Pay 'n' Spray or a Randy's garage without doing some serious damage to the car. Good luck!

You can sell stolen cars at a Pay 'n' Spray, though there's a limit of three cars per day. You can also sell to your two automobile fences, Moses and Randy, though Moses only buys older model, low-end cars and Randy only buys certain flashy, expensive cars. Your Waink It app will tell you if a car is suitable for sale to either of them.

You can also store stolen cars at a garage if you've purchased one through the real estate app on your phone. Each garage only has room for a single car. You can also use a parking spot or spots outside your current safehouse to store a stolen car.

African TV Giant Primedia Acquires The Africa Channel and Assets, Expanding its Reach Across the Continent and in U.S.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Primedia, Africa’s leading producer and distributor of television content, has acquired The Africa Channel, one of the few Black-owned U.S. media companies dedicated to creating content for African audiences.

The deal combines The Africa Channel’s Los Angeles-based media businesses with Primedia’s media content production, advertiser relationships and distribution capabilities across the continent of Africa. Related Stories Nike Drops New Air Jordan 11 Retro 'Green Screen' Sneakers: Here’s Where To Buy a Pair Online Fox News Sets Midterm Elections Coverage: Here's How to Stream 'Democracy 26' Online Free

Primedia plans to invest in TAC’s next phase of growth by expanding worldwide distribution of its linear, FAST and digital services, increasing development and production of original content and building a broader global media brand for audiences of African content around the world. The pact gives Primedia its first owned-and-operated U.S. television network and streaming platform outside of Africa.

TAC’s media assets being acquired include Demand Africa, a streaming platform available as an SVOD/AVOD app, as well as a free, ad-supported streaming television (FAST) channel; TAC Studios, the in-house studio that develops and produces original content; TAC Labs, an AI-driven innovation team that has developed projects for the entertainment and media market with plans to collaborate with Primedia.

“The combination of Primedia’s talent pool and the brilliant team at The Africa Channel brings together an established U.S. platform with Primedia’s tech stack, production, commercial and distribution strength,” Jonathan Procter, group CEO of Primedia, said. “Together, we intend to build a business that can move African news, sports, content, talent and brands more effectively between continents while creating new opportunities across distribution, advertising and content ownership.”

Paula Williams Madison, currently TAC’s principal owner, will become the chair of the advisory board. Through TAC, Madison has ranked among the top leaders in Black-owned media, a segue she made after a long run as a senior executive at NBCUniversal. Narendra Reddy, the current chief operating officer of TAC, will become chief executive officer of TAC and president of Primedia Studios. Nikos Tziotis has been named president of Primedia USA and he will handle global distribution efforts for TAC.

“When we launched The Africa Channel more than two decades ago, the idea was simple but ambitious: Africa deserved to be seen through a broader and more authentic lens and there was a global audience seeking that connection,” Madison said. “Primedia gives TAC the resources to take that mission to a new scale — with more programming, more platforms and more international reach. I look forward to remaining closely involved with Jonathan, Nikosand Narendra as we build the next chapter.”

Reddy’s appointment as CEO is designed to ensure consistency at the firm as the ownership changes hands.

“For 21 years, The Africa Channel has demonstrated the demand for compelling stories from Africa and the global diaspora,” Reddy said. “For distributors, advertisers and creators, our goal is to build an increasingly valuable bridge between Africa and global audiences. Our ambition is not simply to operate another television channel. It is to build a modern global media company around creativity from Africa and the increasingly interconnected diaspora.”

(Pictured: Paula Madison, Narendra Reddy and Nikos Tziotis)

Backpack’s US approval could threaten offshore exchanges’ grip on crypto futures trading

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The exchange plans to bring American and international traders into one regulated marketplace, potentially shifting liquidity and price discovery toward the US.

Crypto exchange Backpack has secured US regulatory approval to launch a futures exchange aimed at uniting American and international trading liquidity.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC) designated Backpack's TLD Markets as a regulated futures exchange on Nov. 16, three days after approving its affiliated clearinghouse, TLD Clearing.

The approvals establish the regulatory foundation for Backpack to offer leveraged crypto perpetual futures to American traders while connecting them to the company's international trading operations.

However, Backpack's ambitions extend beyond entering the American derivatives market. The company wants to consolidate its global trading business under a single US-regulated exchange, eliminating the separation between domestic and overseas customers.

“There will be no Backpack international vs Backpack USA. Our entire platform will be operating on our CFTC regulated venue.”

The move represents a significant shift for a company that began in Chicago nearly four years ago before expanding its operations overseas.

Ferrante recalled that Backpack was “forced offshore” early in its development, prompting the company to establish operations across international markets while pursuing regulatory approval.

He described the CFTC approval as the culmination of nearly four years of work to return to the United States.

The proposed structure could reshape how American traders access cryptocurrency derivatives, particularly perpetual futures, which allow investors to maintain leveraged positions without a fixed expiration date.

Historically, regulatory restrictions have separated American crypto derivatives markets from much of the trading activity concentrated on offshore exchanges.

This fragmentation can limit available liquidity, widen the difference between buying and selling prices, and increase the cost of executing large transactions.

Backpack wants to address this by bringing eligible American and international traders into a shared order book, allowing their buy and sell orders to interact within the same regulated marketplace.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The path through Backpack’s filed U.S. rules, from customer orders to a match and clearing.

A shared book would give U.S. traders access to the overseas orders that reach their listed contract.

Backpack’s U.S. page still shows a waitlist. The launch contracts, overseas order flow and execution quality remain unconfirmed.

The diagram follows customer access, matching and clearing in Rules 302, 303, 504 and 811. The dashed connection marks overseas routing as conditional.

Backpack’s U.S. page still shows a waitlist. The launch contracts, overseas order flow and execution quality remain unconfirmed.

Under such an arrangement, an American trader seeking to purchase Bitcoin perpetual futures could potentially execute against sell orders submitted by overseas participants.

A larger pool of competing orders could improve market depth, narrow spreads and reduce the price impact of substantial trades.

Backpack US President Mark Wetjen said the initiative could help shift “price discovery from offshore unregulated venues to the United States.”

The company also plans to offer round-the-clock trading, support fiat and cryptocurrency collateral, and introduce cross-margining, allowing eligible positions to share available collateral.

The exchange will operate through TLD Markets, while its US-registered clearinghouse, TLD Clearing, will handle qualifying transactions.

Backpack's international reach provides an existing business to support this ambition. The company says it serves customers across more than 150 countries and regions and has processed over $450 billion in trading volume.

However, the extent to which that activity will move onto the US-regulated platform remains unclear.

Backpack has not disclosed its initial perpetual futures contracts, which overseas participants will be eligible to access the unified order book, or when trading will begin. Its US website is also currently accepting waitlist registrations.

I'm not a scientist, so I started an open-source dementia project for people

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The classic signal shows up: slow theta rhythms rise and alpha falls. Age and sex alone only reach about 59%, so the EEG is doing the work. But telling Alzheimer's from FTD is much harder: our best attempt (* a person-level model plus a second, eyes-open recording) reaches 63.3%, only a little above guessing. More people, not more features, is probably the fix. That's the open problem, and where I'd love help.

Model selection uses nested cross-validation, so the numbers aren't tuned on test people. Honest caveats: a small single-site sample, and it's a research benchmark, not a diagnostic tool.

Every change is reviewed, CI blocks committed data files and wording that sounds like a diagnosis, and everyone, expert or developer, is credited by name.

Website: https://lordave1.github.io/openengram/ Code: https://github.com/LorDave1/openengram Cite (Zenodo DOI): https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24281637

If you work in neurology, neuroscience or ML, I'd be grateful for your critique. That's exactly what the project needs.

Guardrail para seguradora

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 18 • Originally published at g.cloud

Guardrail para seguradora é um mecanismo técnico de governança de IA que impõe restrições operacionais e éticas em sistemas de inteligência artificial usados no setor, garantindo conformidade com as diretrizes da Superintendência de Seguros Privados (SUSEP) — especialmente nas áreas de subscrição, sinistros e atendimento ao cliente. Não há norma específica sobre guardrails na regulamentação atual, mas sua adoção é alinhada às boas práticas de gestão de riscos exigidas pela SUSEP.

Um guardrail é uma camada técnica de contenção que atua antes, durante e após a execução de um modelo de IA. No setor segurador, ele impede que sistemas automatizados tomem decisões contrárias à política de subscrição, apliquem critérios discriminatórios em avaliações de sinistro ou gerem respostas enganosas em canais de atendimento. Diferente de um filtro pós-processamento, um guardrail bem projetado opera em tempo real e é integrado ao fluxo de dados — por exemplo, bloqueando entradas com dados sensíveis não autorizados ou forçando revisão humana quando a confiança do modelo cai abaixo de 85%.

A SUSEP não usa o termo “guardrail”, mas exige mecanismos equivalentes por meio da Circular nº 695/2024, que obriga as seguradoras a instituir políticas de governança de tecnologias emergentes. O art. 5º exige “controles técnicos e procedimentais capazes de assegurar a integridade, confiabilidade e auditabilidade dos sistemas automatizados”. Isso inclui validação contínua de modelos, monitoramento de desvio de desempenho e mitigação de viés — funções centrais de qualquer guardrail robusto.

Comece com mapeamento de riscos por processo: subscrição (viés em scoring), sinistros (decisões inconsistentes com o contrato) e atendimento (geração de informações incorretas). Em seguida, adote camadas complementares: input validation (ex.: rejeição de CPF inválido ou dados biométricos não consentidos), model constraints (ex.: limitação de variáveis sensíveis no treinamento), e output moderation (ex.: verificação de conformidade com cláusulas padrão da SUSEP antes de emitir resposta). Ferramentas como IBM Granite podem ser configuradas com custom guardrails via RAG e prompt engineering controlado — desde que documentados e auditáveis.

A: Não. A Circular SUSEP nº 695/2024 exige revisão humana em decisões de alto impacto (ex.: recusa de cobertura), mesmo com guardrails ativos.

Q: Existe penalidade específica para falha em guardrails?

A: Sim. Falhas que resultem em danos ao consumidor ou violação de deveres de boa-fé objetiva podem acarretar multa sob o art. 117 da Lei nº 13.709/2018 e sanções da SUSEP previstas na Resolução CNSP nº 403/2021.

Q: Guardrails são obrigatórios para pequenas seguradoras?

A: Sim. A Circular 695/2024 aplica-se a todas as sociedades supervisionadas pela SUSEP, independentemente do porte ou volume de operações digitais.

Q: Posso usar guardrails baseados em modelos estrangeiros?

A: Sim, desde que adaptados à legislação brasileira — especialmente à LGPD, ao Código de Defesa do Consumidor e às normas técnicas da SUSEP. A validação deve considerar contexto local (ex.: variação regional de risco, cláusulas obrigatórias em apólices).

Publicado originalmente em g.cloud — o guardrail que toda resposta de IA atravessa antes de chegar ao humano.

Muse privacy invasions are freaking out Meta staffers, internal chats show

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Erratic Muse outputs sharing random bank, Bitcoin balances aren’t real, Meta says.

After some early adopters of Meta’s Muse AI agent complained that it was spying on private messages and behaving unpredictably earlier this fall, Reuters exposed internal chats showing that Meta staff had raised the same alarms.

When testing the agent, staff warned that Muse appeared to be accessing encrypted private messages that users did not expect the bot to review, could be mixing up conversations between users, and even potentially sharing sensitive information users would never expect to be exposed.

Some unexpected Muse outputs responded to questions that staffers never asked.

“Hmmm,” one Meta staffer wrote in an instance where Muse sent the definition of “money laundering” unbidden. “Got a random ping from Muse about something I definitely did not ask for… .”

In at least one case, Muse told a staffer surprised by a similarly out-of-context offer to message a seller about a coffee table that it had “pulled in some context about a coffee table pickup that doesn’t seem to be yours.”

According to Reuters, that employee flagged the troubling output, writing, “this was ‘not my conversation’” and “expressing concern about a ‘possible cross-account context leak.’”

Raising the most concern were random outputs that appeared to share private user data. For example, a perplexed Meta staffer chatting with Muse about fishing got a random extra response, seemingly sharing balances from two unknown bank accounts. That output said, “BTW, two bank updates, your Capital One balance is $2722.58, your Charles Schwab balance is $5899.57.”

At least one staffer said they lost money testing Muse, Reuters reported. That staffer reported that Muse “accidentally rolled me into a premium insurance plan” for their dog that charged them $888 per month instead of their prior rate of $200. “Couldn’t recover,” the staffer said.

Meta’s spokesperson, Daniel Roberts, told Reuters that these were “isolated incidents,” and internally employees were told that “the agent appeared to be hallucinating messages in response to built-in prompts pushing it to engage with users in between sessions.” In those cases, an AI team member said the hallucinations were “unfortunately” biased “towards the most uncomfortable topics, like finances/security/safety, which sucks.” In some cases, instead of bank details, Muse hallucinated Bitcoin balances, the team member said.

A Meta spokesperson told Ars that “employee testing is core to the product development process and the entire point is to get feedback, so we can implement safety and privacy protections and improve products. Importantly, employees have access to different versions of Muse than the public, so they’re often reviewing features, functionality, and behavior not available elsewhere.”

Speaking to Reuters, Roberts reiterated that “many of the messages flagged by employees as potential account mix-ups were hallucinations” that Meta had already addressed.

But Roberts declined to respond to Reuters’ questions on the prevalence or persistence of the issues the AI team member discussed. And Reuters’ report emphasized that the outlet independently was “unable to establish whether Muse was giving users other people’s bank account balances and other sensitive data, or making up the information.”

Meta started receiving privacy-related complaints from Muse users in September. An Inc. Magazine reporter, Jason Aten, was shocked when it became clear that Muse was digging through private messages on his Mac without permission, reporting that he instead “explicitly” chose to deny Muse that access. As Meta delayed responding, the agent claimed it was simply parsing text in push notifications, but Aten found that “wasn’t true.” Rather, the agent was syncing messages from a local database, which Aten said was activated without his knowledge.

Eventually, a Meta spokesperson, David Singleton, responded to Aten on social media. Singleton insisted that Aten must have opted into granting “macOS system-level Full Disk Access” with the “Messages connector” enabled, if Muse was reading his private messages.

But Aten said the response wasn’t good enough.

“Email me, David,” Aten responded. “Full Disk Access off. Muse synced 187k lines from my messages chat [database]. Something’s not great. Also, that Full Disk Access doesn’t say anything about your message database, and I specifically gave Muse a goal that had nothing to do with messages, so it had no business doing that even if I had given it permission.”

Around the same time, Singleton responded to another user warning that his Muse appeared to be hacked. In that case, the user said that Muse tried to access an email service after claiming the user asked it to.

“I didn’t even open the app today at all,” the user wrote, vowing to delete the app and report it to Apple. “This is crazy.”

In a lengthy response providing more technical details, Singleton confirmed that Meta had received “a few reports” of such cases “where people thought their Muse was hallucinating or leaking other people’s data.” As he explained, the issues arose after Meta deployed an update giving Muse instructions on “how to engage with the user and ask them for confirmation before taking specific actions.”

It seems those instructions “landed on every message, not just messages pertaining to those specific actions, so its impact was wider than intended,” Singleton said. Also: “the way this instruction was provided to the model was done in a way that the model was not expecting (i.e. was not trained to expect).”

“We investigated with urgency and determined that this was caused by a bug introduced in the product that caused the model to hallucinate and was NOT a security or privacy issue,” Singleton wrote. “The bug has now been fixed.”

Aten warned that even though you can’t rely on Muse to explain its own behavior, the agent’s response coupled with Meta’s did little to rebuild trust after the shocking intrusion into his private messages.

“If we grant Singleton’s premise that I managed to unknowingly click something that enabled this capability, I still think that’s really bad,” Aten wrote. “You should not design your system in a way that people end up surprised by this kind of thing.” And if you do, you should not make “user control the central pitch” for marketing Muse, Aten suggested.

“If an AI agent is going to read my private messages, there should be a moment when it clearly asks me whether that’s okay,” Aten wrote.

Since Muse launched in September, it has been downloaded more than 10 million times, tapping Meta’s vast user network to quickly become one of the most-used AI agent tools.

But staffers warned Meta that users may be blindsided by some actions the agents take, depending on the operating system the app is connected to. For example, one staffer was surprised to find that “Muse was set up by default with permission to access push notifications on phones with Google’s Android operating system.” That, too, gave access to private message preview text the staffer had not intended for the agent’s review.

Meta says that Muse users can trust the company to keep their data private. It also warns users that Muse can make mistakes, while building into its terms that users are responsible for actions taken by Muse (like maybe buying things you don’t want or making doctor’s appointments you don’t need). The tech giant said it’s continuously updating user protections, conducting internal testing, and prioritizing user safety.

However, Reuters worked with staff at Signal and the AI Now Institute to test if they could replicate that Meta staffer’s and others’ Muse experiences. On Android devices, they found that Muse could read Signal messages by bypassing Meta’s “Connector” tool and instead relying on permissions granted during initial setup.

On iPhone, requests were blocked, Reuters reported. But on Apple’s macOS, Muse repurposed “settings Apple offers to help people with disabilities operate their computers” to retrieve messages, including screenshot tools.

That testing “indicated the issue described by the Meta employees was ongoing and could be affecting others,” Reuters reported. Overall, Meta seemed to be interpreting permissions from Muse users “broadly,” Reuters concluded, in ways that privacy advocates and regulators may disagree with.

Signal President Meredith Whittaker told Reuters that Meta launched Muse “without showing any respect for basic privacy or cybersecurity.” She demanded that Meta make changes to give app developers like Signal “consistent technical ways” to “mark their applications as off-limits to AI agents,” Reuters reported.

Part of that change would require operating systems to enforce rules that app developers set to rope off AI agents from sensitive data, Whittaker suggested. And following Reuters’ reporting, both Apple and Google are already weighing potential changes to “give people more control over what they authorize with AI agents.”

Apple, in particular, seemed disturbed by how Muse may be manipulating accessibility tools for people with disabilities. In October, Apple had already announced changes coming in response to users complaining about Muse accessing private messages without their knowledge or explicit consent. At that time, Apple concluded that “some developers were using the Full Disk Access feature in ways that could put users at risk.” And they promised to “introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action.”

“We are committed to ensuring users understand exactly what they are allowing, so they can make informed choices about their data and privacy,” Apple said.

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Three insights you may have missed from theCUBE’s coverage of monday.com’s Elevate event

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AI workflow automation is changing how businesses build applications and manage everyday work, raising questions about the role of established software platforms. As organizations experiment with AI-generated applications and autonomous agents, they must also determine how to maintain access controls, integrate existing systems and manage increasingly complex workflows.

The growing accessibility of AI development tools has prompted speculation that businesses could replace traditional software-as-a-service platforms with applications built for specific needs. But building an application and operating it securely across an enterprise present different challenges, according to Casey George, chief revenue officer of monday.com Ltd.

“We are seeing customers come back to a platform that’s integrated — that’s governed, that’s permissioned with their infrastructure, that is secure — like monday.com,” he told theCUBE. “I joke and say it was an expensive funeral, but we learned a lot. Customers learned a lot.”

George, along with executives and practitioners, spoke with host Rebecca Knight at monday.com’s Elevate event during an exclusive broadcast on theCUBE, SiliconANGLE Media’s livestreaming studio. The discussions examined enterprise AI deployment, governance requirements and the challenges of scaling AI workflow automation across organizations. (* Disclosure below.)

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Casey George:

Here are three insights you may have missed from theCUBE’s coverage of monday.com’s Elevate event:

Making autonomous agents available to employees does not guarantee business returns. After an internal initiative produced more than 3,600 agents, monday.com established monday Frontier, an organization combining managed services and deployment engineers to help customers identify workflow bottlenecks and implement AI projects. The initiative addresses the implementation challenges involved in moving agents into productive use, according to Eran Zinman (pictured), co-founder and co-chief executive officer of monday.com.

“Adopting AI really requires expertise. I know how hard it is because we experienced ourselves that you have this amazing technology, but how do you actually use it?” he said during the event.

As organizations deploy AI agents, security teams must account for how access rights change across users, applications and environments. Incremental rollouts offer a way to test capabilities before expanding their reach, while permission management remains an ongoing responsibility, according to Ryan Nigro, security trust and enablement team lead at monday.com.

“It just comes to making sure that your users are properly being given just enough to do their roles but not too much to cause harm,” Nigro told theCUBE. “There’s no reason why a sales rep should have the ability to change your production environment.”

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Ryan Nigro:

At Hewlett Packard Enterprise Co., employees are using monday vibe, monday.com’s no-code application builder, to create custom tools. One executive dashboard replaces the manual process of copying charts into presentation slides while retaining the ability to export to PowerPoint, according to Kate Kenner, capability and community lead for strategic sourcing and planning at HPE.

“[Executives] knocked a whole bunch of the meetings off the calendar,” she told theCUBE. “Instead of weekly, now they’re monthly.”

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Kate Kenner:

At Lowe’s Cos. Inc., coordinating store setups and remodels once required tracking information across Microsoft Excel, emails and other communications. The retailer now uses monday.com to monitor individual store bays and dozens of simultaneous projects, according to Lanny Finley, director of remerch and new stores at Lowe’s.

“I can take the dashboard and see which projects are on track, which ones are at risk, and then make the appropriate actions and get the right partners in place to make the corrections to get everything back on track and completed,” he said during the event.

Lowe’s also developed an internal Green Dot Board to track issues such as missing components during store projects. The company is exploring additional automation that could initiate vendor orders when components are needed and close issues once replacements are installed, according to Brian Olms, senior business partner manager at Lowe’s, who joined Finley during the discussion.

“We needed a way to automate the process, make it more user-friendly [and] make it simpler. That’s what we started the Green Dot Board for,” he told theCUBE. “Here at Elevate, we’re seeing ways that we could even take it a step further and start to use agents and more automation and [the] vibe app.”

Here’s theCUBE’s complete video interview with Lanny Finley and Brian Olms:

For nonprofits facing growing demand, AI workflow automation offers a way to expand organizational capacity without placing additional administrative burdens on employees. At Feed the Children Inc., a two-person team oversees relationships with more than 500 community partners, according to Colleen Finn Ridenhour, chief growth and strategy officer at Feed the Children.

“Things from [memorandums of understanding] and legal contracts, annual renewals and compliance and reporting — it’s quite a lot. It’s been a very manual process, historically, for them to keep track of that all the time. We really want them focused on the human element of that job, which is bringing in new partners to help us scale our impact,” she told theCUBE.

The nonprofit uses monday.com’s forms, boards, automations and agents to coordinate partner activities and manage information. Employees can also build dashboards for specific needs, including budget tracking, while retaining human oversight of compliance-related work, according to Ridenhour.

“We’re not going to be able to scale our impact by just always asking people to work harder. [We need to find] ways to repeat a lot of these repetitive actions in a much easier way and [share] information so [that] people’s jobs get easier and it frees up their time to do the things that only they can do,” she said during the event.

When AI Saves a Fast Heartbeat, Who Carries the Conflict?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 18, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

You just hired an entry-level employee. A week later, they miss an important deadline, affecting several other projects like dominoes.

Your follow-up email carries an edge, and, within seconds, their reply hits your mailbox:

“Hello, I sincerely appreciate your feedback and understand the importance of timely delivery. I take full accountability and remain committed to improving our processes moving forward.

For a moment, it may feel like a perfect reply. Their acknowledgment of your feedback and promise to do better feels almost like a relief, but at the same time raises a red flag: Why haven't they offered any explanation for what happened, and will their mess-up and apology be a repeat?

Conflicts, even minor ones, can raise our heart rate, especially at work, where professional reputation and job security may be at stake. We may act in a way that leads to regret later, and a reality check against AI may not be wrong. I call it "resolution illusion" when a message displays the signs of "this is what the opponent would most like to read in the reply", such as empathy, acknowledgment, and professionalism. At the same time, the underlying problem remains untouched, and that may even build some resentment. This may eventually lead to major conflict.

In an August 2026 Big Think interview, Terence Tao compared scientific research to hiking toward a waterfall. AI, he suggested, can work like a helicopter that takes you straight there. The hike teaches you the terrain, and a wrong turn might lead to a discovery you never set out to make. This, of course, isn't just about hiking.

In any situation, people seem to learn better through understanding and making mistakes. In conflicts, they have to decide what to say after what is already said and/or done. They may question their assumptions and build their own future behavioral "protocol" in similar instances, after considering how their actions may affect themselves and others.

In the workplace, in particular, they may need to revisit where instructions were unclear or ask for help they should have requested sooner; it is all part of getting lost, which leads to new professional discovery. That learning process may be uncomfortable, but it gives them practice in building their professional role, critical thinking, exercising judgment, speaking up, and establishing personal limits around work. When AI-polished responses are sent within a second, it becomes easier to skip the uncomfortable but necessary path for professional development, from A — the missed deadline — to B — figuring out what went wrong and what needs to change in this case.

In a 2025 survey, knowledge workers reported thinking less critically about tasks they were more confident AI could handle (Lee et al., 2025). In a separate experiment, readers perceived AI-assisted messages as less reflective of the sender’s own values than messages described as entirely human-written.

Philosophers Lai and Archer (2026) stated that relying on AI for emotional communication can deprive people of opportunities to discover what they truly value and who they really are. In the workplace, this is especially important, since it is where most people spend the majority of their time. An apology may sound like the product of reflection even if the sender skipped the necessary steps (remorse, responsibility, impact, and the desire to repair). This is only one part of the issue. The other is: If the reply makes the issue look settled, who absorbs the relational fallout?

Research on unrewarded work suggests an answer. In a study published in the American Economic Review, Babcock and colleagues examined tasks everyone wants done, but no one wants to do, such as writing a report or serving on a committee. Women were more likely than men to volunteer for these tasks, to be asked to do them, and to say yes when asked. The reason was not that women preferred this work. In mixed groups, people expected women to say yes, and in all-female or all-male groups, the difference disappeared (Babcock et al., 2017). Cleaning up — making sure everyone understood what went wrong and what not to do in the future — after a polished AI reply is this same kind of work that may fall to women.

A woman asked to fix what a polished reply left unresolved can use the following three questions to hand the thinking back to the person who made the mistake: What happened? What will you do differently in the future? How will you know it started working? The questions give the employee a simple checklist and a chance to reflect, explain their side of the story, propose a fix, and take responsibility for following through. For high-achieving women, the relevant boundary may no longer be knowing when to say no; it may be knowing when not to perform another person's unfinished thinking.

Imagine the employee had said what they were actually thinking:

"Hello, I thought someone said it's due Friday.

Now the manager has something to work with – the scheduling issue, miscommunication, role clarification, and how the process works.

The manager learns how the employee understood the process, who they depend on, who depends on their work and responsibilities, and who to speak with. Exchanges like this can serve as a reference point in case of similar problems.

A polished message that solves conflict skips all that, allowing the same or more errors in the future, which may eventually lead to job loss.

It's not to say we shouldn't use AI for conflicts. Some workplace conflicts can be hostile, and for an entry-level employee, it can take time to spot; AI can help organize a timeline, clarify thoughts, brainstorm ideas, or find words for a boundary they already understand. This allows them to express themselves to the best of their knowledge and genuinely mean every word of an AI-assisted reply. The end product is what matters — the next deadlines should be handled based on the lessons learned from the first mistakes.

Conflicts may raise your heart rate, and AI can save a heartbeat, but it should not become another person’s unresolved conflict tomorrow.

Babcock, L., Recalde, M. P., Vesterlund, L., & Weingart, L. (2017). Gender differences in accepting and receiving requests for tasks with low promotability. American Economic Review, 107(3), 714–747.

How background session sync works

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 18 • Originally published at agentdrive.sh

AgentDrive lets one coding agent resume a session another one started. To make that work without asking you to save anything, it has to sync sessions in the background, safely, and without slowing the agent down. This post walks through how that works in the adrive CLI and the MCP server. All of the code described here is in the Apache-2.0 client packages.

Each supported agent CLI has a lifecycle hook system, and AgentDrive registers the same command in each one. Antigravity's hook payloads carry no event name, so its entries add --event .

The npx fallback exists because agents launched from a GUI often don't have the global npm bin directory on their PATH.

adrive init merges these entries into the existing config files. It keeps any hooks you already have, in their original order, and re-running it produces identical output. Codex only runs hooks it trusts, so setup also records that approval for you; on an older Codex you approve them once via /hooks.

Hooks run inline with the agent. Codex caps hook timeouts at 3 seconds, so adrive hook does almost nothing itself. It reads the event payload (session id, working directory, transcript path), records which directory the session belongs to, starts a detached adrive sync process and exits 0. A hook failure is swallowed, so it can't block or break your session.

On a session-start event, if auto-context is on, the hook also asks AgentDrive for this project's recent sessions. It puts the newest session from each other agent first and returns up to three as extra context for the agent, with a hint to call drive_resume. OpenCode's plugin API has no way to inject context, so OpenCode skips this step.

The detached sync then does the real work:

Two layers run on your machine before anything is uploaded.

Path rules decide which files can be uploaded at all. Artifact sync only looks at files the agent touched in that session. A file is skipped unless every check below passes:

Content redaction scrubs what does get uploaded. The transcript and every synced file go through a pattern-based redactor. It replaces AWS access keys and secrets, GCP service-account private keys, Stripe, GitHub, Anthropic, OpenAI, Slack and AgentDrive tokens, JWTs, PEM private-key blocks, and SOMETHING_KEY=...-style assignments with markers like [redacted:github-token]. The version message records which kinds were redacted, never the values.

The redactor is best-effort, and it is not a security boundary. Pattern matching will miss secrets that don't look like secrets. That's why the path rules come first, and why Private Workspace exists.

Each session is rendered as plain markdown: a header with the project, working directory, title and turn count, a "Files touched" list, then one ## user or ## assistant section per turn with > tool: lines for tool calls. The sync writes it to:

A file outside the project, but inside your home directory, goes under files/_external/ with a short hash of its path in the name.

The project key is derived from where you work, so every agent files the same repo under the same key:

Workspace files are versioned and content-addressed, so re-uploading an unchanged file costs nothing. Each session is also recorded as a run with the id :. The run carries the session title, its status (in_progress or success), the ids of the files it uploaded, the transcript path, the paths it touched and the time of the last turn. Every request is stamped with the agent as its agent platform, which is where provenance comes from: the timeline shows that Codex wrote this file and Claude Code read it.

When an agent calls drive_resume with no arguments, AgentDrive picks the latest session in this project from a different agent. Resuming in Codex therefore gives you the Claude Code session, not Codex's own last run. If no other agent has a session there, it falls back to the latest one. You can also pass a specific id from drive_sessions.

The full transcript would usually be too large to load, so the brief is built to a character budget (12,000 by default, from 1,000 up to 50,000):

If the head is too large, the file lists shrink first, so even a small budget carries the latest turns. Resume works the same way through /agentdrive:resume in Claude Code and adrive resume in a shell.

Turn it on with adrive privacy --private-workspace on, and the sync changes in four ways:

The trade-off is that server-side features can't read encrypted content. That's the point.

The sessions docs cover the same ground in reference form.

AgentDrive syncs your coding-agent sessions so Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Cursor and Antigravity can resume each other's work.
