Skyrim's Most Iconic Region Is Making a Major Comeback

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Elder Scrolls Online is heading north in 2027, bringing a familiar snowy destination to its expanding map

Winter is coming to Tamriel, and The Elder Scrolls Online is preparing to take players somewhere particularly familiar. One of the most memorable regions from The Elder Scrolls 5: Skyrim is making its way to the MMORPG, giving fans another reason to revisit the icy northern reaches of the continent.

Bethesda has revealed that Winterhold is coming to The Elder Scrolls Online in early 2027 as part of Season Three. The new major zone and accompanying storyline will expand ESO's Second Era map, bringing the snow-covered region into the online RPG for the first time.

While Bethesda hasn't shared many details about what players can expect to do in Winterhold, it has confirmed that more information is on the way. A special livestream dedicated to Season Three and the new adventure is planned for January 2027, with further details expected in the developer's December community letter.

Winterhold is a remote, snow-covered region in the northern reaches of Skyrim, overlooking the Sea of Ghosts. Its namesake city was once a prosperous settlement, but much of it was destroyed during the Great Collapse, a mysterious disaster that caused large sections of the town to sink into the sea. The region is perhaps best known for the College of Winterhold, a prestigious institution dedicated to the study of magic.

In Skyrim, players can explore the College and join its ranks, embarking on a major questline involving mysterious artifacts, dangerous magical experiments, and the Eye of Magnus. But there's more to the region than its famous school of magic. The surrounding landscape includes icy coastlines, snow-covered mountains, ancient Nordic ruins, caves, and settlements inhabited by hunters and other hardy residents.

Winterhold is a particularly atmospheric corner of Skyrim, blending ancient Nordic history, magical intrigue, and some of the game's harshest wilderness.

Exactly which parts of the wider region will be included in The Elder Scrolls Online remain unclear. However, ESO takes place centuries before Skyrim, giving players an opportunity to explore Winterhold during a very different period in its history.

Not Micron, Not Sandisk. This Is My Top Memory Stock for 2027.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

SK Hynix is bigger than Micron and Sandisk in the DRAM and NAND flash markets, which is why it has the potential to outperform them next year.

By Harsh Chauhan – Nov 18, 2026 at 2:01PM EST

Micron Technology and Sandisk have sizzled on the stock market in 2026. Both stocks have delivered impressive gains this year, despite witnessing a significant pullback in recent months amid the broader correction in tech stocks.

Micron stock, for instance, has gained 128% in 2026, as of this writing. Sandisk's gains are even bigger at 194%. Importantly, both memory companies can step on the gas once again in 2027, driven by the artificial intelligence (AI)-fueled demand for compute and storage.

However, I think that Micron and Sandisk could be overshadowed by South Korean memory giant SK Hynix (SKHY -1.23%) next year. Let's look at the reasons why.

SK Hynix sells both dynamic random-access memory (DRAM) and NAND flash memory. Counterpoint Research points out that the company controlled 29% of the DRAM market in the third quarter of 2026. It is second only to Samsung in this market. Micron, for comparison, has a 21% share of the DRAM market.

Meanwhile, SK Hynix's 19% share of NAND flash puts it ahead of both Micron and Sandisk, which have market shares of 15% and 11%, respectively. The South Korean company's healthy market shares in both DRAM and NAND flash explain why it reported phenomenal growth in Q3. SK Hynix's revenue surged 328% year over year to $79 billion, easily beating the consensus estimate.

The company's operating profit jumped 632% from the year-ago period and exceeded analysts' expectations. SK Hynix is well-placed to sustain such impressive growth. That's because the company has already sold more than half of its production capacity for 2027 and 2028 under long-term agreements with 14 customers.

Moreover, SK Hynix anticipates that memory demand will outstrip supply through 2030, indicating that the favorable environment fueling the company's stunning growth is sustainable. Also, SK Hynix is ramping up production of its next-generation high-bandwidth memory (HBM) chips, known as HBM4.

It points out that HBM4 has twice the bandwidth of the previous-generation HBM3E chips and 40% higher power efficiency. SK Hynix notes that HBM4 revenue and shipments will surpass those of HBM3E in the current quarter, which should bode well for the company's margins. That's because HBM4 reportedly carries a 24% premium per gigabyte (GB) over the previous generation, HBM3E.

What's more, SK Hynix controls a third of the global HBM market, higher than Micron's 18% share. This is another reason to buy SK Hynix, considering that the global HBM market's revenue could surge to a whopping $641 billion in 2030, according to Bloomberg Intelligence. The firm has significantly upgraded this guidance from its earlier estimate of $260 billion, noting that the average price of HBM will more than double over the next four years.

So, there is a strong likelihood that SK Hynix's revenue growth will outpace Wall Street's expectations.

SKHY Revenue Estimates for Current Fiscal Year data by YCharts

SK Hynix's lead over Micron and Sandisk in DRAM and NAND flash should ideally give it greater pricing power over its rivals. This probably explains why analysts expect SK Hynix's earnings per share to increase at a much faster pace in the long run.

SKHY EPS LT Growth Estimates data by YCharts

Also, SK Hynix's valuation is in line with Micron and Sandisk's multiples.

So, even though SK Hynix stock hasn't made a bright start on the U.S. stock market following its listing on the Nasdaq this year, it remains worth buying on account of its red-hot growth, cheap valuation, and the ability to outperform Micron and Sandisk. In the end, I won't be surprised to see this AI stock's fortunes turn around in the New Year, and buying it while it trades at cheap multiples seems prudent.

Fed officials expect more rate hikes, worry about AI impact on inflation, minutes show

📅 2026-11-18  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Federal Reserve officials expressed confidence in October that further interest rate increases will be needed to stem inflation, though they decided to hold off while gathering more information, according to meeting minutes released Wednesday.Policymakers put artificial intelligence at the core of their discussions, indicating that demand associated with the technology would be "a material source of inflation risk." More broadly, officials said "inflation pressures in the economy were broad based" and would warrant action, with "some" policymakers advocating for a quarter percentage point hike at the meeting. A couple participants even noted the possibility that a larger increase might be needed at the next policy meeting in December.The meeting summary said "almost all participants judged that, under their baseline outlooks for inflation and the labor market, further action to raise the target range for the federal funds rate … would likely be appropriate, in order to ensure a timely return of inflation to the 2 percent objective."In a CNBC interview Wednesday, Fed Governor Christopher Waller reiterated his belief that the Federal Open Market Committee should consider a half-point hike in December.

Officials at the meeting expressed a high level of concern over AI, both as a source of inflation and financial instability. Fed staff members who briefed participants noted "that off-balance-sheet leasing commitments and circular financing arrangements among AI-related firms could amplify losses if returns on AI investment were to disappoint.""Most participants observed that AI-related debt had grown rapidly and that much of this growth had taken place through complex and opaque financing structures," the minutes said. "A few participants remarked that AI-related exposures were becoming increasingly concentrated among a small number of firms."Officials said they still expect inflation to fall off sharply in 2027 and beyond as the impact from the current shocks wear off.Ultimately, the FOMC voted 10-2 to keep its benchmark overnight borrowing rate locked in a range between 3.75-4%. Governor Lisa Cook and Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack dissented in favor of a quarter-point increase. Those voting in favor of the hold "pointed to benefits in accumulating additional information, which would help in determining the policy stance appropriate to ensure a timely return of inflation to the Committee's 2 percent goal."

Since the meeting, the inflation data has showed that the pace of price increases remains well above target.

The consumer price index in October rose 0.4% for all items and 0.3% for the core that excludes food and energy. Both readings were 0.1 percentage point higher than expected.

While indicating concern over how stubborn inflation has been, most Fed officials who have spoken publicly advocated a patient approach.

However, Waller said Wednesday that the Fed may have to "shock" markets by approving a half-point hike in December. He is the only central banker to espouse that belief publicly, though St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem said he anticipates as much as a full percentage point move higher by the spring of 2027. As governor, Waller is a permanent FOMC voter; Musalem will not vote until 2028.

Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee said Friday on CNBC that the central bank risks losing its credibility if it doesn't act.

At the core of policymakers' concern is whether rising prices related to the Iran war and President Donald Trump's tariffs, in many cases temporary events that wouldn't prompt a response, are instead becoming more entrenched.

Markets are fully pricing in a quarter percentage point hike at the Dec. 8-9 meeting that includes low odds of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch gauge. Futures traders also are pricing a solid likelihood of two more increases in 2027.

Christopher Nolan Reveals Why Watching 'The Odyssey' at Home Is Better than IMAX

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Oscar winning director explains how repeat viewings reveal new layers in his cast's performances and shares his home theater tips.

The Oscar winning director explains how repeat viewings reveal new layers in his cast's performances and shares his home theater tips.

Christopher Nolan has spent much of his career championing the theatrical experience. From shooting on IMAX film to encouraging audiences to see his movies on the biggest screens possible, the director has made no secret of his belief that movies are meant to be experienced in theaters.

But that doesn't mean Nolan thinks watching a movie at home is a lesser experience. In fact, the filmmaker believes home viewing offers some advantages that even the most impressive theater can't replicate. And with The Odyssey arriving on digital, 4K Ultra HD and Blu-ray on Nov. 17, he's encouraging audiences to take advantage of them.

Speaking with Polygon ahead of the film's home release, Nolan discussed how watching a movie in your living room can change the experience, particularly when it comes to appreciating the work of the actors.

"The fun thing about watching a film in the home is a different kind of concentration," Nolan tells Polygon. "When you go to a movie, you're in the dark, you're really focused for a couple hours. In the home, it's a different type of concentration because you can pause the movie, you can go get a snack, come back."

That freedom means viewers engage with a movie differently than they would during a theatrical screening.

"It does allow you to control the timeline and really look at the detail of things," he said. "And for me, it's really about the performances."

While The Odyssey features enormous ships, mythical creatures, and elaborate action sequences, Nolan believes the film's greatest rewards on repeat viewings may come from paying closer attention to its actors.

"The human element is what connects you with the story," Nolan says. "But I think the work of these actors, this cast, this incredible cast in the film, it rewards multiple viewings."

Nolan knows that better than most, having spent countless hours reviewing footage during the editing process. He pointed to the layers in performances by Matt Damon, Anne Hathaway, Tom Holland, Robert Pattinson, and Samantha Morton as details that audiences might appreciate more fully when watching again.

"There's subtleties and nuances in what they're doing that I, in the edit suite, get to watch again and again and again and sort of pick up on all the different things that Matt or Anne or Tom or Rob, what they're bringing to things, or Samantha Morton," Nolan says. "It's really fun for people to be able to look at that in the home and see all the different layers that these guys have put into the story."

When I mentioned that my wife and I enjoy discussing movies while watching them at home, something that isn't exactly encouraged in a theater, Nolan acknowledged the experience has its own appeal.

"It's a different form of engaging with the story, and it has strengths and it has weaknesses," he says. "Nice to be able to sit on your own couch and stretch out, you know what I mean? It's been really exciting over the course of my career to see the leaps forward in terms of technology, in terms of what you're able to do in the home."

Nolan also appreciates how much home-viewing technology has improved during his filmmaking career.

"It's been really exciting over the course of my career to see the leaps forward in terms of technology, in terms of what you're able to do in the home," he says.

And for anyone interested in bringing a little more of the theatrical experience into their living room, Nolan has a suggestion.

"I'm a big fan of projectors," he said. "You can get a relatively low-cost, pretty high-quality projector, darken your living room, maybe introduce some popcorn into the equation or whatever. Have a mini movie theater and do it on your own timeline. That's tremendous fun."

But Nolan doesn't believe a movie necessarily loses its theatrical identity once audiences begin watching it at home. In fact, he suggests that the excitement surrounding a major theatrical release can follow a film into people's living rooms.

"When you have a movie that's played, as The Odyssey has, at length in movie theaters, even viewed in the home, there's a kind of excitement that comes with an event like that," Nolan says. "Even if people are seeing it for the first time, I think the film sort of carries that with it."

The Odyssey arrives on digital, 4K Ultra HD and Blu-ray on Nov. 17

An AI Debt Collection Service Exposed 6.6 Million Accounts and 315,000 Call Recordings

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Two vulnerabilities in the India-based AI-driven debt collection platform CreditNirvana exposed 6.6 million accounts and about 315,000 call recordings from the company's agentic call center, according to research released on Wednesday. Owned by the Indian fintech company Perfios, the platform is built to “read a borrower’s full context, decide the next best action, and execute it autonomously, continuously, and within policy.”

The bugs were patched in CreditNirvana's systems in June and were disclosed publicly in October. But independent researcher Eaton Zveare, who found and disclosed them, notes that, while the vulnerabilities were active, they exposed personal information on millions of borrowers. Details about financial collections are inherently—perhaps obviously—sensitive, because they relate to unpaid accounts or debts and often involve extensive documentation of people's personal histories.

“They fixed it in one day, which was great, but this is really a pretty significant case,” Zveare tells WIRED. “Some of the largest fintech firms across India use this company to autonomously collect debts, so they naturally hold data, including credit scores, call recordings, transcripts, court records, and other sensitive documents. There’s just so much in there that makes it a very sensitive issue.”

CreditNirvana and Perfios did not return WIRED's requests for comment. Zveare says he worked directly with India’s Computer Emergency Response Team (CERT-In) to get the vulnerabilities fixed. CERT-In categorized one vulnerability as “high” severity and the other as “critical” severity.

The high-severity vulnerability impacted the call center data, exposing hundreds of thousands of calls as well as recommendations for next steps generated by the platform after calls were complete. It could also have been exploited to allow an attacker to manage AI agents within the platform and direct their own agentic campaigns.

The critical severity vulnerability impacted CreditNirvana's main platform, and Zveare says it specifically exposed the data of three corporate customers—all prominent Indian financial services firms—using CreditNirvana for their collections. The data also exposed millions of individuals’ accounts, which contain credit reports, ID cards, case management details, and legal documents, along with certain types of communications, including millions of emails, texts, and call transcripts related to financial services, unpaid accounts, and collections. The vulnerability also could have been exploited by an attacker to track the location of human debt collection agents and for some administrative privileges within the system.

“Successful exploitation of this vulnerability could allow the attacker to enumerate platform users, establish sessions as arbitrary users including administrative users, access and modify sensitive information with the privileges of the impersonated user,” CERT-In wrote.

Zveare says he is relieved that the issue was resolved quickly, but is conscious that the bugs were potentially present in CreditNirvana's systems for years. As AI is integrated into more and more automated systems, synthesizing data from a greater number of sources and using it to draw conclusions, Zveare says, the episode serves as “an important example of an AI-powered service having a very sensitive data exposure.”

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The seat-shaking theater tech is supposed to make audiences feel part of the action. But then came 'Wicker.'

The seats move. The wind blows. Water sprays you in the face. That’s 4DX, the movie theater format designed to make audiences feel like they’re inside the action. Usually, that means a chair-shaking car chase or superhero battle. For those watching Wicker, it meant being bucked in their seats every time Olivia Colman had sex with a basket.

The dramedy about a fisherwoman who commissions a basketmaker to weave her a husband became the first romance to get the treatment since the format was developed in 2009. Applying that technology to Wicker was “a special case,” says Erik Dane, vp digital marketing at CJ 4DPLEX, the company behind the format, because “in the world of 4DX, there are not that many erotic scenes.” Related Stories Movies 'Wicker' Star Alexander Skarsgard Will Be Pushed Differently for Golden Globes Than Other Awards (Exclusive) Business Alexander Skarsgard to Narrate Quinn Audio Erotica Original Set in World of 'Wicker'

Judging by one viewer’s account, they’ve figured it out — even if it’s murder on the janitorial staff. “The seats start thrusting,” posted Tumblr user cranberrymoons in her online review, describing how moviegoers were “covered in the popcorn and diet coke … flying everywhere.” When the onscreen couple broke the bed, the seats jolted along. “My hair is in my face and I’m holding onto the arm rests of my seat trying not to fall out,” she wrote. Afterward came “a beat of stunned silence … while everyone catches their breath.” Then, “the whole room starts cheering and clapping.”

He Lives in Madonna’s Old Mansion. Now He’s Made a Movie About Cambodian Genocide

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Taz F. Stark used to run the hottest club in London. Today, he’s a first-time director whose harrowing short film is wowing folks like Al Pacino, Bill Nighy and Rufus Swell.

By Benjamin Svetkey and Julian Sancton, Editors

Taz F. Stark has always known how to get a bunch of movie stars into the same room. He’s the former London nightlife impresario who in 2017 opened Laylow, the red-hot Notting Hill club where Rihanna partied, Mick Jagger unwound after a Stones show and Robert Downey Jr. privately screened an Avengers movie.

But now Stark, 41, is pulling off something far more impressive: He’s getting movie stars to watch his movie about the Cambodian genocide. His directorial debut, a harrowing 14-minute short called Child of the Rouge, has been earning raves from A-list fans like Al Pacino, who screened the film for friends; Rufus Sewell, who recently introduced it at a showing at the British Consul-General residence in Hancock Park; and Bill Nighy, who’s lined up for a London screening. Related Stories Music Boy George on His Controversial Israel Trip, Ed Sheeran and the Friends He Lost Along the Way Movies Basque Cinema Expands Its (Rapidly) Growing Footprint in Tallinn

“Directing and the hospitality business aren’t all that different,” Stark tells Rambling. “In both, you’re managing crazy people. But it has a similar magic. You gather a team of creatives, and they all get together and make something.” In this case, that all-star team includes exec producer Brady Corbet (Oscar-winning director of The Brutalist), Dhani Harrison (George Harrison’s son, who helped compose the score), and Olivia Neergaard-Holm (editor on The Apprentice). “My gaffer did The Big Lebowski,” Stark adds. “My AD was straight off CSI.”

Stark, who grew up in Britain, spent a chunk of his 20s teaching English in Cambodia, which is what inspired the film. He wrote the screenplay — it follows a couple delivering their baby in an abandoned hospital during Khmer Rouge’s seizure of Phnom Penh in 1975 — on a flight from London to L.A., where he now lives with his girlfriend, actress Stacy Martin, in Madonna’s old castle above the Hollywood sign. Soon after landing, he was introduced — by his dog — to the guy who’d produce the picture. “I was at this coffee shop, and our dogs started playing,” he recalls of his first meeting with Joe Russell, whose credits include the Marisa Tomei-Minnie Driver short Laboratory Conditions. “It was canine intervention.” Before long, they were shooting in a warehouse in Los Feliz decked out as a Cambodian hospital, with François Chau (Lost) and Pisay Pao (Z Nation) as the leads.

“The biggest difference between directing and hospitality,” Stark notes, “is that with restaurants, all your staff want to be doing something else. They all want to be writing and directing and acting. When you’re on a film set, everybody’s just grateful for the job they have.”

Stark’s film starts streaming on Mubi on Dec. 7.

Will the Kennedy Center Honors Go Full Trump?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The once star-studded D.C event is moving to new digs next month. Will the president’s name come along?

By Benjamin Svetkey and Julian Sancton, Editors

Never mind who’s onstage at this year’s Kennedy Center Honors. Keep an eye on the banners.

Donald Trump’s efforts to put his name on the performing arts institution continue to be thwarted by a federal court. But this year, while the Kennedy Center’s main building is closed for renovations — and still, after five months, remains shrouded by a tarp hiding the building’s original signage — the Dec. 7 honors ceremony is moving to Washington’s Capital One Arena, where the judge’s no-name-changing order may not apply. Related Stories Business Trump Says Federal Film Tax Credit Is Gaining Momentum Movies Mind the Gap: British Producer-Financier Phil Hunt Is Bringing Head Gear Stateside

“The injunction may only block Trump from affixing his name to the physical building that’s owned by the federal government,” says a source familiar with the Kennedy Center’s inner workings, noting that it remains “a very good question” as to whether there will be “Trump-Kennedy Center” branding at the Capital One event. After all, we know how much the president loves top billing.

If Trump’s name does end up on posters and programs, it’s unlikely the recipients will object too strenuously. This year’s crop of honorees — David Mamet, Paul Anka, David Foster, Andy Garcia and Barbara Mandrell — ranges from outspoken Trump admirers to, at least publicly, Trump agnostics. Mamet has enthusiastically championed the president; Anka performed a modified version of “My Way” at Mar-a-Lago (“When we vote your way”); and Foster hosted a fund-raiser for Trump-backed L.A. mayoral candidate Spencer Pratt.

“In normal times, the board was 50-50 Democrat and Republicans,” says the source. “But Trump is now chairman of the board, so he’s making the final decisions.”

Also in normal times, honorees were traditionally announced in midsummer. But this year, like last, they came months later. The holdup in 2025 was apparently because of Tom Cruise, who kept the board dangling while considering the invitation before finally deciding he had “scheduling conflicts.” It’s unknown who, if anyone, kept the board hanging this year, but they still haven’t announced a lineup of presenters and performers. Those names could provide an even more revealing measure of Hollywood’s willingness to participate in the Trump-Kennedy Center Honors — or whatever they end up being called.

Taz F. Stark used to run the hottest club in London. Today, he’s a first-time director whose harrowing short film is wowing folks like Al Pacino, Bill Nighy and Rufus Swell; The 4DX seat-shaking theater tech is supposed to make audiences feel part of the action. But then came Wicker.

Elizabeth Meriwether is set to receive the 2027 Showrunner Award at next year’s ATX Television Festival, Variety has learned.

Meriwether will take part in a conversation onstage at the festival, which runs June 3-6, 2027, in Austin, TX. It will be her first time appearing at the festival.

The Showrunner Award is meant to recognize those who “foster an environment of collaboration and creativity in their role as showrunner, and whose creative voice has a singular and expansive influence on TV,” per the official description. Past recipients include David E. Kelley and Bill Lawrence. Meriwether most recently created and served as showrunner on the Hulu series “Furious” which starred Emmy Rossum as an FBI agent hunting a serial killer played by Lola Petticrew. The show was loosely inspired by the 1987 film “Black Widow” and was renewed for a second season in August.

She is also behind the Emmy-winning Hulu liminted series “The Dropout” starring Amanda Seyfried, which delved into the life and career of Elizabeth Holmes. Her other credits include the acclaimed series “Dying for Sex” at FX.

We’re thrilled and honored to have Liz join us next summer, and to celebrate her remarkable career thus far.”

Morgan Jay, the stand-up comedian best known for his viral auto-tuned crowd-work songs, has launched a new show on YouTube called “Music Therapy.”

The interview series, which debuted on Wednesday with first guest Kam Patterson (“Saturday Night Live”), is part talk show, part improvised jam session. In each episode, Jay inhabits the role of a musical therapist, blending unscripted, intimate conversation with games and live songwriting. At the end, each guest will create a song or jingle with Jay. And part of who I am is who I surround myself with,” Jay said in a statement. “I couldn’t be happier to work with Hello Party on this. We have such a great collaborative team, and I know we will make something new and unique in the space.”

“Music Therapy” is developed and produced by Austin-based creative agency Hello Party and financed by Live Nation Studios, which also provides access to its talent roster for guest appearances.

“The industry is changing, and agencies that want to stay relevant in the creator economy are going to have to pivot toward actually being creators,” said Ty Stafford, Hello Party’s founder and CEO. “‘Music Therapy’ is proof that branded entertainment can start with a great idea and, with the right partners in place, become something audiences actually want to watch.”

“Music Therapy With Morgan Jay” was created by Jay and Stafford and is directed by Stafford. Brooke Schley serves as showrunner and co-writes the series with Jay. Executive producers are Stafford, Graham Sicalowski, Robin Rapino, Stella Bulochnikov, Brooke Schley and Matt Sadeghian.

Jay was named one of Variety‘s 10 Comics to Watch in 2025. He has become one of the fastest-growing comedians in recent years, amassing more than 20 million followers across social platforms. That virality has translated to hundreds of thousands of tickets sold and a recent comedy special, “Live at the Village,” which is streaming on Hulu.

As an actor, Jay’s credits include “Love Language,” “Stick,” “St. Denis Medical” and “Wonder Man.” He’ll next appear opposite Pete Holmes in the indie feature “The Big Kill.”

Rose Byrne To Head 2027 Tropfest Jury

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

EXCLUSIVE: Rose Byrne will head the jury at the 2027 Tropfest.

The long-running short film festival will take place on February 13, 2027, in The Domain in Sydney. Byrne previously served as the Tropfest jury head in 2017.

This year, the Tropfest films will stream globally on YouTube. The festival will feature as a week-long cultural event titled TropWeek. Programming across the week includes a three-day educational program at the National Institute of Dramatic Art (NIDA) exploring the craft and business of filmmaking, supported by Screen Australia and YouTube. Related Stories News Broadway's 'Fallen Angels' Live Capture Starring Rose Byrne & Kelli O'Hara Sets Theatrical Release News Jenna Ortega & Rose Byrne Are Getting 'Nasty' For Warner Bros Clockwork & LuckyChap

The week culminates in a free day-to-night festival in The Domain on February 13, with live entertainment, followed by the evening screening of 16 world premiere shorts. Competition winners will be announced live on the night and via the YouTube livestream. Watch on Deadline

Tropfest film submissions open on Tuesday 1 December and close on Tuesday 5 January 2027. Filmmakers are required to create a short film of seven minutes or less featuring the 2027 Tropfest signature item: a necklace.

Prizes for winning filmmakers include a share of the CommBank Tropfest Emerging Filmmakers Fund’s $100,000. First, second, and third prizes will be $50,000, $30,000, and $20,000, respectively.

The Tropfest Foundation, a not-for-profit organisation launched in 2025, is led by a board comprising Chair Sarah Murdoch, Bryan Brown, Peter V’landys, Richard Weinberg and John Polson. Britt Wickes serves as Executive Director.

“I have such fond memories of watching great shorts at Tropfest with thousands of people under the stars in my hometown of Sydney,” Byrne said in a statement. “I’m thrilled to be back to lead the jury in 2027 and can’t wait to see what stories Australia’s next generation of filmmakers will tell.”

Revolut Lists GoMining Token for UK and European Users

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home » Exchanges » Revolut Lists GoMining Token for UK and European Users

The move brings the niche utility token to more than 15 million fintech app users under the EU’s MiCA regulatory framework.

Retail fintech giant Revolut has added GOMINING, the native utility token of the digital Bitcoin mining platform GoMining, to its in-app cryptocurrency trading service. The listing enables over 15 million Revolut crypto users across the United Kingdom and the European Economic Area (EEA) to buy, hold, and trade the token alongside everyday banking services.

Prior to this integration, GOMINING was primarily accessible through crypto-native exchanges such as Kraken, KuCoin, and Bitget.

The listing builds on an existing payment integration established in 2024 that allowed European users to fund GoMining accounts via Revolut Pay. It also adds GOMINING to Revolut’s cryptocurrency trading service.

Revolut reviews digital assets before listing them, assessing their risks, compliance requirements and potential financial crime exposure. It also monitors listed tokens on an ongoing basis as part of its procedures under the EU’s Markets in Crypto-Assets Regulation (MiCA).

“For years, there has been a clear line between crypto and traditional finance, and most people stayed on one side of it,” said Mark Zalan, CEO of GoMining, in a statement announcing the listing. “For GoMining, this listing means our token is no longer only for people who already live in the crypto world.”

GOMINING has several uses within the GoMining platform. Token holders can use it to pay digital miner maintenance fees, with discounts of up to 20%, buy digital miners on the platform’s marketplace and purchase boosts in Miner Wars, GoMining’s mining game.

The token also has a governance function. Users can lock their GOMINING tokens to receive veGOMINING, which gives them voting rights on the allocation of ecosystem rewards.

Revolut customers can now buy GOMINING directly through the app. To use the token on GoMining, they must first transfer it to an external GoMining account.

The Revolut listing gives GOMINING exposure to a wider retail audience through a mainstream financial app. It also provides another way for users to acquire the token without first opening an account on a crypto-native exchange.

However, buying GOMINING through Revolut does not provide direct access to GoMining’s platform features. Users must transfer their tokens to an external wallet before using them within the GoMining ecosystem.

Istruzioni per l'uso del calciomercato: dallo scouting al moneyball e l'AI, tutto quello che c'è da sapere

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Il calciomercato è il nostro core business, d'accordo. Ma è pure quel concetto astratto che tiene svegli i tifosi durante nelle fasi operative e che alimenta i sogni dei bambini. Dietro le notizie c'è però un mondo che molti non conoscono e che nella giornata di oggi proveremo a raccontarvi nel dettaglio, partendo dai metodi classici per acquistare un giocatore per arrivare ai nuovi modi di fare, sempre più creativi, di agenti e direttori sportivi. Spoiler: l'intelligenza artificiale ha fatto il suo ingresso prepotente anche in questo mondo.

Il mondo delle trattative ha funzionato così per decenni. Impersonificato dagli storici direttori sportivi italiani, basava il suo fondamento sui contatti personali dei vari dirigenti di club. Rapporti più o meno stretti con agenti e intermediari, rete di collaboratori in giro per il mondo e scouting. Tanto scouting. Alcune società ci hanno costruito sopra una fortuna, scovando sui campi di periferia talenti poi diventati icone mondiali. Concetti che esistono ancora ma che stanno piano piano scomparendo.

Nell'ultimo decennio il calcio ha incontrato i dati e le sfaccettature proprie del mondo finanziario. Mettendo tutto nel pentolone ne è uscito fuori il Moneyball, di derivazione dal baseball. In soldoni si tratta di andare ad individuare le inefficienze del mercato, comprando il "prodotto" sottovalutato e non il grande nome. Significa, semplificando ancora, comprare a 5 ciò che in realtà vale 20. In questo ha sempre più peso l'utilizzo dell'AI, con i suoi modelli predittivi che hanno fatto irruzione nelle stanze dei bottoni dei vari club, non solo di Serie A.

C'è poi chi si affida al momento storico per indirizzare le strategie. C'è chi qua e là nel tempo ha cercato di costruire degli "instant team", squadre che puntano su nomi e giocatori di conclamata esperienza per provare a vincere tutto e subito. Raramente questa strada ha funzionato, e infatti si vede sempre meno. O c'è chi scandaglia il panorama mondiale alla ricerca dei migliori parametri zero: un modo semplice per abbattere il costo dei cartellini in favore di ingaggi (e commissioni) più corpose.

MCNC Chief Operating Officer Tommy Jacobson receives Order of the Long Leaf Pine

Broadband and networking pioneer honored with North Carolina's highest civilian recognition for more than three decades of public service and leadership

RESEARCH TRIANGLE PARK, N.C., Nov. 18, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Tommy Jacobson, Senior Vice President and Chief Operating Officer of MCNC, today has been awarded the Order of the Long Leaf Pine, the most prestigious honor presented by the Governor of North Carolina for exemplary service to the state.

The award recognizes Jacobson's more than 32 years of dedicated leadership in expanding high-speed connectivity, closing the digital divide, and laying the technological foundation for North Carolina's schools, universities, health care systems, and rural communities.

His career of service began in the summer of 1993 when he joined MCNC â a technology non-profit established in 1980 â as a student intern prior to his senior year at NC State University. After graduating in 1994 with a Bachelor of Science in Electrical Engineering, he went on to embody the university's "Think and Do" ethos across three decades of executive, operational, and engineering leadership.

As the chief architect of the North Carolina Research and Education Network (NCREN), Jacobson oversaw the design, deployment, and operational resilience of middle-mile broadband infrastructure serving nearly 1,000 community anchor institutions. Today, the network spans more than 4,500 miles across all 100 North Carolina counties, delivering high-speed connectivity and cybersecurity services to millions of users.

How can districts keep CTE programs relevant as tech evolves?

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The rapid pace of change can make it difficult for even employers to have clear answers for workplace readiness, an industry expert says.

How can leaders of high school career and technical education programs ensure those offerings remain relevant in preparing students to adapt in a rapidly changing workforce?

“That question is one everyone is trying to answer right now. I’m not sure there is one clear answer,” said Alisha Hyslop, chief policy, research and content officer at the Association for Career and Technical Education.

Hyslop said the main piece of advice for educators is to work closely with businesses and industries to ensure programs are preparing students with the skills they’ll need to be successful — and that’s probably the same, at least on the surface, as it would have been 10 or 20 years ago.

But there are important distinctions in 2026, Hyslop said.

Because technology is changing so rapidly, employers don’t always have a clear answer for workplace readiness approaches, she said.

Hyslop said ACTE is also hearing that students need to understand basic, foundational artificial intelligence skills and how to use them. Additionally, students should continue to "build up durable skills," which is "really critical," she said.

Despite employers’ own lack of clarity at times, regular conversations should definitely continue, Hyslop said.

“There should be a give-and-take between the CTE educators, and folks working on curriculum and instruction in CTE, and the employer community,” she said. And although technology will continue to rapidly evolve, “students are still going to need critical thinking and communications skills, and the ability to work in teams, and evaluate information — and all of those foundational skills that apply across technologies.”

CTE programs focused on information technology are changing the most rapidly, although Hyslop said she has seen plenty of research to show that across the spectrum of career fields, new AI tools are being implemented and deployed. One example is the use of the technology in the agriculture field to monitor soil health and predict crop growth, she said.

“We had some examples from a school in Bentonville [Ark.] that was using AI in health science programs. They had created a meal planning app for mothers with gestational diabetes, or for pregnant women who could get answers to specific questions; those were joint projects between healthcare and technology students," Hyslop said. "There were lots of applications in digital media, and lots of ethical considerations that students are exploring as they go through that process.”

While some industries are more impacted by the advent of AI and other changes in the workplace than others, at first glance, people might think it’s mainly an issue in technology classes, Hyslop said. “And that is not the case. Because we’re seeing applications across career areas, once students get into the workplace.”

During a panel discussion at a recent ACTE conference focused on the use of AI in CTE programs, Hyslop said, students emphasized how important it was that they learned foundational skills first.

One student pointed out the approach of using a calculator to solve math problems, she recalled. “But that’s not how we teach students in kindergarten. We teach them basic mathematical functions first, and then introduce the calculator to do more complicated things.”

By learning the foundational level skills in CTE programs, they could evaluate the answers given by AI more competently, the students said. For instance, a student studying automotive mechanics could examine AI-generated instructions on how to fix cars.

“If you had no real understanding of those instructions, you wouldn’t know if it made a mistake,” she said. “That was a really important takeaway from that panel — that even students realized that AI, or whatever the next technology is, is still going to need people to instruct, evaluate and modify all of these technologies.”

Why 2 Pinterest vets and a neurosurgeon are building a Reddit for doctors

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Medicine’s complexity and ever-evolving nature means doctors will always have more to learn and discuss—but in the digital age, there’s also a question of where those conversations can best take place.

Online, healthcare workers’ discourse is distributed across various social media platforms, forums, and group chats. Meanwhile, offline, medical professionals are “hyper-specialized,” with limited opportunities to work with colleagues from other disciplines, according to Dr. Rohan Ramakrishna, chief of neurosurgery at New York-Presbyterian Brooklyn Methodist Hospital.

As a neurosurgeon who specializes in brain tumors, he said, “the doctors I interact with on a regular basis are radiation oncologists and neuro-oncologists, and that’s it.” Even hospital doctors’ lounges are disappearing, he added, removing another opportunity for doctors to socialize and discuss medicine.

So Ramakrishna teamed up with Vikram Bhaskaran and Arun Ranganathan—two Pinterest alums who previously led engineering teams related to creators, content, and partners—to cofound an app that could foster that sense of community. 

Initially, the trio envisioned Roon as a place for doctors to provide patients with information; but in the app’s early days, Bhaskaran said, they found that physicians were especially interested in seeing what their own peers had to say—so they decided to rework the platform to facilitate those interactions.

Now, a year after its launch, the platform is adding features that bring Pinterest’s focus on sharing to the thousands of physicians on the platform to share everything from treatment advice to conference photos. As social media apps shift and online misinformation proliferates, the founders are hoping Roon will help physicians separate the signal from the noise.

Where doctors gather online

In some ways, Roon’s concept echoes that of #MedTwitter, which became a widely used, informal hub for medical experts and others to exchange information, ideas, and opinions, creating “a completely open-source environment that democratizes medical information,” as two physicians wrote in 2021. The hashtag still exists, and some continue to use it, but Twitter’s transition into X—and the accompanying changes—scattered some of  #MedTwitter to other platforms.

But medical science is always evolving, Ramakrishna said, as are politics and policy changes, and doctors lack a “home” for making sense of it all. Roon is “a place where you have net new knowledge creation, because people are trying to unpack what it all means, and understand it together.” 

To help shape Roon’s knowledge-creation network, the company enlisted more than 400 “founding physicians” from a range of specialties and institutions, who the company said “are owners with equity in Roon.” 

“A year ago, we had kind of a thesis: that physicians should have a place online where they can collaborate, connect, and that was safe and private,” said Bhaskaran, and it’s “been really heartening” to see how physicians are now using Roon. 

Creating community for a core audience

Roon joins a growing number of communications platforms designed for clinicians. Doximity, a networking service that offers an AI scribe and other tools, says more than 85% of U.S. physicians are registered members. There’s also Sermo, Figure 1, Mednet, Doquity—which is used by clinicians across much of Asia—and other new ventures like Marit Health, where medical professionals can share salary data. 

Each of them varies in form and function. Roon, which has the benefit of being designed by Pinterest veterans, is built to look and feel like mainstream social media sites. On it, users discuss new research, policy changes, and health topics that emerge in the news cycle, Ramakrishna said, and ask for advice on patient care. For example, he said, a family medicine doctor who posed a question related to reproductive health and the pituitary gland got replies from peers in family medicine, OB-GYN, high-risk maternal-fetal medicine, neurosurgery and neurology. 

There’s also an ability to form private groups—for example, Ramakrishna said, there’s one for chief medical officers, and another for residential program directors—and a new, Pinterest-board-inspired “Collections” feature that lets users group related content and media together. 

Selective, intentionally

Roon also differs from most medical communications apps in that, for the time being, it’s exclusive to physicians—M.D.s and D.O.s—who are licensed in the U.S.

Bhaskaran says the criteria mean that Roon can function as a place to share expertise built in the field—something that augments published literature in an important way. 

“There’s so much of medicine that’s outside of the published literature, that’s not accessible via chatbot or via AI: that’s in people’s heads,” he says. 

Roon currently has tens of thousands of users, Bhaskaran says, noting the goal is to reach 250,000 by the end of next year. That would constitute almost a quarter of the 1.08 million physicians that were licensed in the U.S. as of 2024. 

Part of the challenge of growing is the fragmentation of physicians across various social media apps. Some physicians also say they value the diverse perspectives offered by other healthcare workers, like nurses and pharmacists, and by those in other countries. 

They do plan to invite certain non-physician specialties, like infectious disease scientists, Bhaskaran added, but they still have work to do to build up the community of physicians, which remains their first goal. They also plan to expand Roon internationally, “but again, very thoughtfully, because verification is just a little bit of a hard problem once you leave the United States.” In the meantime, the company has a waitlist for medical professionals who’d like to join.

Human amid AI

Roon currently employs AI for tasks like filling out physicians’ profiles, routing users’ questions to specialists, and generating summaries of content. Its team is also working on new AI-powered functions to keep users up-to-date, like an audio digest feature that summarizes medical news and related Roon posts.

Then, there’s the training question: a database of medical information based on real-life expertise would be valuable for training LLMs, and physicians on X have expressed concern about it being used that way. A company spokesperson said Roon “currently has no plans to sell its data,” though the company may use it to refine its own models, and hasn’t made a “definitive commitment ruling out all future uses involving third-party LLMs.” 

But in the face of AI’s all-consuming nature, “our thesis is actually [that] the human network will become more valuable over time, especially as most of the world goes, ‘hey, we’re unclear who wrote this content,’ or ‘everything you see on the internet is AI slop,'” Bhaskaran says. ”We’re taking kind of an old school bet—which is, there’ll be value for community, and that’ll transcend any specific technology.”

‘No one asked for this’: AI is being pushed on people—and they’re sick of it

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The AI era is fully here—and while companies bill it as a solution to problems, caveats like mass layoffs, environmental effects, privacy concerns and the sloppification of culture have many people fed up with something they never desired in the first place.

The technology faces growing resentment from workers, who are being urged to embrace AI tools even as companies have rushed to reduce their workforces in favor of automation. 

Online, many posts about AI have a common refrain: no one asked for these tools or the disruptions they’re causing. 

“Nobody asked for Google to be worse—but AI made it happen,” one X user recently remarked. “No one asked for computer hardware costs to rise—but AI made it happen. No one asked for human customer service to be replaced—but AI made it happen.”

In another post, Sarah Hoyt, a science fiction, fantasy and mystery author, said she doesn’t hate AI, but is “begging all the crazy people out there to stop pushing AI where no one asked for it.”

The disenchantment plays out in the data: In 2019, 81% of Glassdoor reviews mentioning AI were positive, the company said, compared to 43% this year. Meanwhile, Pew Research found that 52% of Americans were more concerned than excited about AI, up from 37% in 2021; and in an Ipsos poll from June, 67% of respondents thought AI would worsen the worker experience.

“The closest analog is the replacement of physical labor throughout the long Industrial Revolution,” said Jiemin Tina Wei, an assistant professor of history at Florida International University. But in this case, it’s “brain work,” rather than manual labor, that’s being replaced. 

“And now, the industries that are getting allegedly wiped out are the ‘low-hanging fruit’ of white-collar work,” she said.

But livelihoods being gutted isn’t the only reason many are anti-AI.

The productivity paradox

Louis Hyman, a historian at Johns Hopkins University who specializes in labor and the American economy, points to a “productivity paradox”: a contradiction that was also generally true of the rise of computers in the ‘80s, in which similarly dizzying investments didn’t necessarily make people better at their jobs as promised.

Hyman points to snags like the frequency of software updates: that “every time you learn how to do something, suddenly they break it, and you have to relearn it.” With the introduction of AI, it’s “as if all software is being updated, all at once, everywhere.” 

Another part of the equation, he said, is that it isn’t clear how some people are using AI tools.

Hyman said he uses AI to write software and automate “boring tasks”—for example, he wrote a script to rotate the thousands of photos he takes in archives so they all face right-side-up. But, he said, “most people are not wanting to sit down and write Python code to automate their job. They just want to do their job.” 

Added to that is the deep fear of being replaced once the work is automated.

“I’m a tenured professor—I literally can’t be fired,” he said. “If I automate some boring part of my job, that just means I have more time to do the more interesting parts of my job. And that’s not true for all people, right?” 

A new occupational hazard

Another reason many people push back on AI is the lack of adequate guardrails—especially when it could cause major issues at work.

In one case, an AI tool deleted a software company’s entire database. In another, AI was blamed for deleting an entirely edited season of a Swedish reality show mere weeks before the scheduled premiere. In medical settings, AI scribes have put inaccurate information in patient records, raising the question of who would be held liable for resulting medical errors. 

Meanwhile, misreads by AI identification tools used by law enforcement have led to wrongful arrests and injuries, per the MIT AI Risk Initiative. Other AI-generated errors have popped up in court documents, federal reports, news stories and fast food drive-thrus. 

“The main point I’ve pushed and will continue to push re: AI is ‘no one asked for this,’ ” radio host Adam Xii said on X, in response to news that Anthropic hallucinated details in their defense during a music copyright lawsuit. “So when a product or service no one asked for suddenly becomes ubiquitous, one must ask: why? Because AI tools are meant to generate more wealth for the wealthy at the expense of creatives.”

At the least, AI’s convenience is being offset by a need to check its work that has created new opportunities for human error. The proliferation of “workslop” even led a tech company’s CEO to fully ban AI use, as Inc. reported this summer. 

“A lot of what I’ve seen is kind of annoyance at the proselytizers at work—or the people who are heavy adopters—creating a lot of extra work for the people around them,” because those people downstream then have to check the work, said Wei.

Wei said that content moderation and data annotation work “can be extremely mentally taxing.” Research has found that “good” challenging work will be both difficult and interesting to certain extents, she said. But checking output is both difficult, because you have to look for every small potential error, and boring.

“You’re not producing knowledge, but merely supervising the production of words, knowledge, content,” Wei said.

The unwanted technological advances continue

AI’s intrusive nature—accompanied, again, by the threat of replacement—is reflected in workers’ behavior, not just in fed-up tweetstorms. One report found that nearly a third of workers surveyed, and 44% among Gen Z, had tried to sabotage their companies’ AI strategies.

Section, an AI planning and upskilling company,  recently reported that 69% of workers surveyed said their organization had encouraged the use of AI agents, but only 16% actually used them. Thirty-eight percent said they use other AI features.

The frustration hasn’t seemed to deter tech companies.

“We heard you wanted Copilot Mode at work,” read an X post from Microsoft that promoted Edge’s Copilot Mode for Business.

“No, you heard wrong,” a computer technician replied. “Literally no one asked for all this AI. In fact, everyone wants to know how to remove it.” 

Questions of organizations

In some cases, it seems companies are starting to discover that automation has some shortcomings.

In the U.S., 32% of hiring managers who eliminated positions in favor of AI have had to reinstate the same or similar roles, according to research from consulting firm Robert Half. Similarly, Gartner, Inc. predicts that by 2027, half of companies that attributed reductions in staff to AI will rehire people to perform similar work.

Wei said the mismatch between the hopes for AI and the reality of its adoption relates to a quote from Bill Haywood, a famous labor organizer, who said that “the manager’s brains are under the workman’s cap.”

In other words, she said, “it’s really ultimately the workers who possess the knowledge and the know-how to run day-to-day operations. Some people have argued that the history of labor is just managers trying to take back that knowledge in an attempt to replace workers in the production.”

“And I guess some people experience AI that way—that it’s being [imposed] upon them so that they train, ultimately, their final, automated replacement.”

Hyman said that a better alternative to the “adapt or die” mentality would be for organizations to use the technology to empower their employees. They should “actually say, ‘okay, we’re gonna create organizational conditions that let you replace yourself, and then focus on the work that needs to be done’—because the amount of work to be done is not finite, right?”

“I get told on a nearly weekly basis that historians are going to be replaced,” Hyman said. “So, yeah: it depends on how valuable you think getting reliable information and history is.”

He added that a world “where the only source of history or journalism is from either Open AI or Anthropic” would feel like an “Orwellian nightmare.”

“That seems not a great road to go down,” he said.

Boy George on His Controversial Israel Trip, Ed Sheeran and the Friends He Lost Along the Way

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Culture Club singer names the former collaborator who told him “Shame on you” and backs Robert Kraft’s decision to bar Macklemore from Gillette Stadium: “You can take your freedom of speech over there and do it on the street.”

Boy George wrote his new single, “We Will Dance Again,” after visiting the Nova Exhibition in London, an immersive installation that commemorates the Oct. 7, 2023, attacks on the Nova Music Festival that resulted in the deaths of 378 people and 44 people taken hostage.

He did it, he says, to send a message to his Jewish friends: “Not everybody hates you. You are loved.”

The Culture Club frontman, 65, was not nearly prepared for the massive backlash that ensued, which spilled outside of social media into friendships that went back years. Among them was his collaborator Martyn Ware, the founder of New Wave bands like Human League and Heaven 17, who messaged him: “Shame on you.” Related Stories Movies Basque Cinema Expands Its (Rapidly) Growing Footprint in Tallinn TV 'Slow Horses' Actress Ruth Bradley to Be Honored at the Oscar Wilde Awards (Exclusive)

Undeterred, he recently visited Israel and played two sold-out shows at Tel Aviv’s Menora Mivtachim Arena on Nov. 4 and 7. He also met with Israeli president Isaac Herzog and Nova survivors.

With his single now officially released and available on most streaming platforms, Boy George checked in with The Hollywood Reporter on a stop in Chicago.

He opened up about his Israel trip, explained why he used AI to make “We Will Dance Again” and revealed where he stands on the Ed Sheeran/Macklemore controversy that resulted in Sheeran’s entire band walking off of his world tour.

What’s a memory you cherish from the trip to Israel?

One of the sweetest things to me was people from London saying, “Oh, my aunt’s in Israel, can she come and see you? And will you say hello?” And I met a few friends of mine’s families that they haven’t seen for years. And just meeting the Nova survivors, that was probably the highlight of the whole trip, because you suddenly understand why this journey started in the first place.

I saw the Nova exhibition in L.A. It’s a very, very dark exhibit. When you played Israel, didn’t some of the survivors come onstage with you?

Yeah. There was about 13 survivors and also people that lost family. So it was a real mix of people. I think it was really beautiful to let people be seen. And obviously, as a performer, you are uncomfortable because you don’t want to hijack someone else’s experience. You want to show it respect, but at the same time, going to Israel was a massive statement, and I had to be bold about it.

Somebody that reviewed my show said I should have snuck in quietly. I was like, “That wasn’t an option.” And sneaking in quietly right now is not helpful because nobody else is being quiet. So I don’t think it’s the time to be quiet. I think it’s the time to be clear and to be loving. And I’ve made some mistakes in my communication online. There are people I shouldn’t have bothered reacting to.

You’ve said you’ve lost friends over this. Is there anyone you’d name?

Martyn Ware, who was in Human League and Heaven 17. I’ve worked with Martyn. I did a version of “I Wanna Be Your Dog” with him, and we sung onstage together. He came out quite early and said, “Shame on you,” and all of that. I just tweeted back, “You’ve lost that loving feeling. “

Most of them I don’t want to name because I don’t want them to have any more attention than they’ve already had from me.

You’ve said the music industry has been very one-sided on this. Do you find most people in the industry are anti-Israel?

I don’t think they’re anti-Israel. I just think they’re not necessarily informed about history, and it’s become quite trendy to shout certain things out. There’s a lot of pressure to agree with everybody else. Your thought, my thought, are not the deciding factor on what happens in the Middle East. We’ve got nothing to do with it. We’re not going to change it. So what we can do is, in our immediate surroundings, be more loving, be more open, be more kind, respectful, all of those things that you can do.

I was getting messages from friends of mine all over the world. Some people I didn’t even know were Jewish. And I’ve had letters from people. So there is a lot of support out there, but it is scared of itself.

When I saw you speaking up for your Jewish friends on Ireland’s Late Late Show in May, I knew you had something in your heart that needed expressing. I didn’t realize to what extent it would end up expressing itself.

When I decided to make this record, I was sending a message to my friends to say, “Not everybody hates you. You are loved.” And that’s the point I tried to make when I was in Ireland, is that growing up during the punk period, one of my biggest allies was a man called Philip Sallon.

One of the first things he ever said to me was, “You look Irish.” And I said, “I actually am.” And he said, “I’m a Jew and a homosexual.” And that was 1976. And when you’re young and impressionable, you don’t forget those moments. And Philip was a big reason why I got to know so many Jewish people because in London, this one knows that one, that one knows that one.

I can’t sit here and say every single Jewish person I’ve ever met I’ve got on with. Of course I haven’t. But Jewish people are characterful, they’re kind, they’re passionate, they’re funny, they’re bitchy. They’ve got all the qualities that I love.

You said you used AI to make “We Will Dance Again.” What aspect of the song uses AI?

The music I wrote with an AI tool. The melody I [wrote], the words I [wrote] And the reason I used AI was because I didn’t want somebody hanging over my shoulder saying, “Oh, you can’t say that. Oh, that’s too harsh.” And that would’ve happened because I’ve had 40 years of that. Forty years of “That’s too gay, that’s too this, that’s too that, that’s not commercial.”

I make a lot of music. I put out almost 200 tracks in the last two or three years. So I’m always writing. In respect to “We Will Dance Again,” that line, that’s a hook. You hear it straight away. As a writer, you go, “Got it. I got it. OK, there we go.”

I saw a video where you were performing a concert and the music cut out and you kept singing a cappella. I couldn’t tell whether it was intentional sabotage or a technical snafu. What happened?

I think it was a “technical snafu.” I love that word. I don’t think it was deliberate. There was a couple of moments when the mic went and I wasn’t sure whether I’d pulled out the wire. And also the guitar went out in a really pivotal moment, but I kept my cool. We were in the round, so there was nowhere to hide. When you’re in a proscenium situation, you can always dart off into a curtain for a minute, but in this instance, you couldn’t really leave.

What it reminded me of is Ed Sheeran, who went onstage after his band walked out on him and did his show a cappella.

In the moment, you always have to remember that people have paid to see you and that you’re there to perform. You can’t get lost in something, but it can really distract your whole evening. I think it’s really important to go, “No, no, no, I have to get through this.”

Do you have anything else to say about Ed Sheeran in terms of that whole controversy regarding Macklemore being dropped from his tour?

Obviously, the stadium owner, Robert Kraft, I know that he’s Jewish. He spent a long time fighting antisemitism, so he was never going to allow that to carry on where he worked, where he’s an owner.

You have a choice who comes and sits at your dinner table, whether you’re a billionaire or a housewife, you make a choice. And if you’ve cooked the meal and paid for the building, you do have a right as a person, as a Jewish person, to say, “No, you can take your freedom of speech over there and do it on the street.”

I told my editor that “Do You Really Want to Hurt Me” is like a Jewish mother song.

Brad Pitt Wants to Play a James Bond Villain

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The announcement finally arrived this week: Robert Pattinson is James Bond. But before we could even come up for air about Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve’s decision to have arguably the busiest movie star in the business today follow Daniel Craig as 007, the Internet had already moved on. Fans started asking more questions. Who will costar as the next Bond girl? Who’s performing the song? And, most importantly, who would play opposite Pattinson as the villain?

Well, how about Brad Pitt? The F1 star likely didn’t expect a James Bond question when he stepped on the red carpet for the premiere of his latest film, The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth. The spinoff sequel of Quentin Tarantino’s Once Upon a Time... in Hollywood sees Pitt return as stuntman Cliff Booth under the direction of David Fincher, which couldn’t be further from the life of a British MI6 hitman. Still, Pitt was excited to hear for the first time that Pattinson landed the role when a reporter for ExtraTV shared the news. “I love this idea,” Pitt said. “He’s the real deal.”

Would he ever think about playing the villain? Pitt winked at the camera and said, “Robert? Denis? You know what, I’d like to do that. My man Javier [Bardem] did it so well [in Skyfall] It’s tall shoes, but I’d take that on.”

Later down the red carpet, Pitt answered the question again when Complex asked if he had any advice for Pattinson. “Sign me for the villain,” he said, coming around to the idea even more. “Get Denis Villeneuve on the flip phone. Let’s go!”

Elsewhere, Pattinson’s fellow Dune: Part Three costar Florence Pugh is already pitching herself as a potential villain and/or Bond girl. When Villeneuve presented her with an honor at Elle's Women in Hollywood event on Monday, Pugh congratulated the director on finding the next James Bond. “Nice catch,” she said. “Any villains the size of a five-foot-four-inch female?" I’m sure this is just the beginning.

I’ll admit, Pitt wasn’t my first choice for the next Bond villain. If anything, Pattinson was. But I love how enthusiastic he is about the possibility. Now, I kind of feel like I need to see it.

What Is a Thought Made Of? Scientists Reveal Why Human Consciousness May Be Beyond the Reach of AI

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A familiar song can pull you back decades in an instant, and the chemistry behind that rush of memory may hold clues to what separates human minds from AI.

How does the brain turn a collection of memories into thoughts, emotions, and the experience of being conscious? Scientists have spent decades studying how neurons communicate through electrical and chemical signals, yet the physical processes that allow us to recall experiences, attach feelings to them, and give them meaning remain incompletely understood.

A new scientific review suggests that part of the answer may lie in the complex chemistry surrounding brain cells. Rather than viewing memory primarily as a product of neuronal activity, the researchers propose that interactions among neurons, supporting brain cells, and molecules in their surrounding environment could play a fundamental role in shaping thought and conscious experience.

Dr. Gerard Marx of MX Biotech Ltd. and Prof. Chaim Gilon of the Hebrew University of Jerusalem’s Department of Chemistry present their theory in Consciousness, Memory, and Intelligence: A Mechanistic Perspective. Building on earlier research into how experiences might be physically stored in the brain, they examine whether the chemistry of memory could help explain the origins of thought and consciousness.

Their proposal also raises a question about artificial intelligence: If human memory and emotion depend on the chemistry of living cells, can computers ever develop consciousness simply by becoming more powerful?

Scientists have long studied how neurons communicate to form and retrieve memories. But Marx and Gilon suggest that important parts of this process may also occur in the molecular environment surrounding brain cells.

Their model, called the Tripartite Mechanism of Memory, involves three interacting components: brain cells, particularly neurons and astrocytes; the molecular material surrounding those cells; and chemicals such as metal ions, neurotransmitters, and gliotransmitters.

According to the theory, these components could allow the brain to chemically encode and retrieve information. Rather than serving only as a support structure, the environment surrounding brain cells may actively participate in memory storage.

Astrocytes, star-shaped cells that interact closely with neurons, play a central role in the proposed mechanism. The researchers suggest that coordinated activity among these cells, neurons, and surrounding molecules could contribute to how memories are formed and processed.

A familiar song can bring back memories from decades ago, complete with feelings that seemed long forgotten. Such experiences show that remembering involves more than retrieving information.

Marx and Gilon propose that the emotional content of memories may be linked to their chemical encoding. In their model, signaling molecules, including neurotransmitters and gliotransmitters, help connect stored information with the feelings associated with an experience.

The researchers also suggest that retrieving and interpreting these chemically stored memories may contribute to the emergence of thought. In this view, memory is not simply a record of past events but an active process through which the brain assigns meaning to experience.

Computers and brains both store and process information, but the underlying mechanisms differ. While computers rely on electronic hardware, biological memory involves living cells and complex chemical interactions.

Marx and Gilon argue that this distinction could matter for consciousness. AI systems can perform increasingly sophisticated intellectual tasks, but their ability to process information does not establish that they experience thoughts or emotions.

For example, an AI system can identify a familiar song and describe the memories or feelings it might evoke in a person. Whether such a system could actually experience nostalgia is a different question.

The researchers suggest that if consciousness depends partly on the biochemical processes involved in human memory, greater computational power alone may not be sufficient to reproduce it.

The proposed model offers a possible connection between memory, emotion, and thought, but it does not establish how physical processes in the brain produce conscious experience.

The authors acknowledge that consciousness remains poorly understood and do not claim to have demonstrated that AI cannot become conscious.

Instead, their work suggests that investigating the chemical mechanisms of memory could help explain how stored experiences acquire emotional meaning and contribute to thought. Whether those processes are essential for consciousness remains an open question.

Reference: “Consciousness, Memory and Intelligence: A Mechanistic Perspective” by Gerard Marx and Chaim Gilon, 4 August 2026, International Journal of Psychiatry Research. DOI: 10.33425/2641-4317.1251

OpenAI says its AI found 14% gains in AMD’s flagship GPU design

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Some of the model's circuit changes are already in a chip AMD has sent for manufacturing.

OpenAI says its models improved power, performance and area by up to 14% on AMD’s flagship GPU designs, with some changes in a chip sent for manufacturing and single-digit gains on AMD’s next flagship CPU. It also detailed AI-made gains on its own Jalapeño chip and is training a GPT-Synopsys model with Synopsys.

OpenAI says its AI models have improved the design of AMD’s flagship graphics chips, with some changes already in a chip sent for manufacturing. In a blog post on Wednesday, it reported gains of up to 14% in power, performance and area, the three measures chip designers trade off, against AMD’s heavily optimised designs.

The model rewrote the code that describes a chip’s circuits, and used Synopsys software to check each change still worked. OpenAI says this was about a year’s worth of progress in under five months. AMD also confirmed single-digit gains in key parts of its next flagship CPU, according to OpenAI.

OpenAI used the same approach on Jalapeño, its first in-house chip, which went from design to manufacturing in nine months. Changes suggested by its models cut the size of two key parts of the chip by about 13% and 10%, and worked in the finished silicon. Startups are chasing the same idea: Architect Labs raised $24M in June to help companies design custom chips with AI.

OpenAI also tested five generations of its models on designing two small open-source processors. Its newest, GPT-6.2 Astra, scored highest both when improving existing designs and when building a processor from written requirements. OpenAI noted the second test was easier, because models could leave out features the originals had.

Next, OpenAI is training GPT-Synopsys with Synopsys, which is refocusing on AI design, to run the design tools engineers already use. Not everyone is fully convinced yet. OpenAI’s own chip chief, Richard Ho, said last month he still does not fully trust AI design changes, so every one is checked.

Please no servers down on Grand Theft Auto day

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The PlayStation Network isn't down – knock on wood – but PS5 users have reported a rise in errors as the whole world counts down to GTA 6 release time.

Like other PS5 owners speaking up online, UK-based GamesRadar+ writers have encountered issues redeeming game codes and, very specifically, upgrading a GTA 6 edition. (Here in the eastern US, I was able to redeem a different game code without issue at roughly 12:45pm.)

The internet's ever-handy Downdetector notes that "user reports show problems with Playstation Network," with a significant spike in reported errors beginning at approximately 1pm ET today. That would've been around quitting time for much of the UK workforce, putting in my head the image of countless gamers seeing the unprecedented reviews for GTA 6 and running home to secure and pre-load their copy.

On ResetEra, some users who say they're in France, the UK, and neighboring countries claim that their PlayStation Store interface or GTA 6 downloads were sluggish or stalled, but went through after repeated attempts. One of our UK writers says his download also worked fine after brute-forcing his way through code redemption issues. GTA 6 doesn't have any physical discs, so everyone will be plugging in a code at some point.

However, the PlayStation Network status page is showing green across the board at the time of writing, for North America and the UK, suggesting these issues may be isolated or minor.

The last thing that anyone wants, especially Sony, is an obstacle between PS5 players and GTA 6. Some store slowdown is expected given the heavy user load anticipated (remember when Hollow Knight: Silksong crashed the planet?), even with early pre-loading taking some of the day-one edge off, so let's hope this is nothing more than some server hiccups.
