Why your fine-tuning job takes longer than the FLOPs say

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Predicting end-to-end fine-tuning time on shared GPU clusters, from 272 production runs.

A preview of our upcoming paper, Predicting End-to-End Fine-Tuning Time for Generative Language Models. The full version will be on arXiv soon.

GPUs are among the most expensive and scarcest resources in AI today. Anyone running a shared cluster has to decide, every day, which jobs go on which GPUs and in what order. That plan is only as good as the estimate of how long each job will take. When a job finishes hours early, GPUs sit idle until the next one is dispatched. When it runs hours late, everything queued behind it slips, experiments wait and delivery dates move.

Better duration estimates turn this into planning instead of guesswork: what runs, when, and on how many GPUs, with less idle capacity in between. Yet the estimate most teams use is a back-of-the-envelope FLOP calculation. It says nine hours, and the job finishes the next afternoon.

We wanted to know how far off that math really is, and whether we could do better before a job is even dispatched. So we took 272 real supervised fine-tuning (SFT) jobs from our production pool of NVIDIA H200 GPUs, about 7,382 GPU-hours in total, and compared analytical estimates against learned predictors.

The short version: the usual FLOP-based estimate explains little of what we observed (R² = 0.31, MAE 12.41 h). A CatBoost regressor that only sees information available before dispatch reaches R² = 0.98, with a mean absolute error of 1.71 h and a median error of 0.51 h in 5-fold cross-validation. The gap comes from two things a closed-form estimate cannot do. The regressor sees more training-specific signals, such as evaluation cadence, checkpoint policy, per-GPU load and fine-tuning regime, and it learns the nonlinear interactions between them from past runs.

For a dense Transformer, training costs roughly 6·P·D floating-point operations, where P is the parameter count and D the number of training tokens. Divide that by what your GPUs can deliver and you get a training-loop time:

Here N_GPU is the number of GPUs, FLOPS_peak is the peak throughput of one GPU from its spec sheet, and MFU (Model FLOPs Utilization) is the fraction of that peak the training actually achieves. Real jobs never run at peak because of memory traffic, communication and kernel inefficiencies, so MFU typically sits somewhere between 30% and 60%, and the right value for a given job is not known in advance.

This is a good description of the optimization loop. A job is more than its loop, though. Before the first step there is environment setup, model download and loading, and tokenization. During training there are periodic evaluations and checkpoint uploads. At the end, the final model goes to object storage. On a multi-tenant cluster, all of it competes with other jobs for network and storage I/O.

These overheads depend on configuration, and they do not scale neatly with workload or GPU count. Two jobs with the same nominal compute can finish hours apart.

To keep the comparison fair, the FLOP baseline we test counts the scheduled evaluation tokens as well as the training tokens, and we sweep MFU over a wide range.

We treat end-to-end duration as a supervised regression problem. Given a feature vector built from the job configuration and model metadata, predict the wall-clock time between the moment the job gets its GPUs and the moment it is done. Queue time is excluded.

We compared Linear Regression, Ridge, XGBoost and CatBoost. Feature selection combined correlation filtering, mutual information and permutation importance across 18 candidate feature sets, followed by a grid search with MAE as the criterion. The final CatBoost model uses 24 features.

Distribution of end-to-end duration across the 272 runs. Mean 23.43 h, median 15.29 h.

All numbers come from stratified 5-fold cross-validation. The first four rows are analytical and involve no fitting.

No single MFU value fixes the FLOP baseline. We swept MFU from 0.25 to 0.65. The best setting, 30%, still leaves a mean error above 12 hours on jobs that last 23 hours on average.

A more detailed analytical model does not close the gap. As a second baseline we took Calculon (Isaev et al., SC '23), an analytical performance model for LLM training from prior work. It builds an explicit layer graph and accounts for compute, memory traffic and inter-device communication. We extended the public implementation to cover LoRA, grouped-query attention, fused attention, bfloat16 and SwiGLU. Its estimates still correspond to a median of only 11% of the observed wall-clock time. That says nothing against Calculon as a step-level tool, which is what it was designed for. It shows how much of a production job happens outside the optimization step.

Linear models help, and then stall. Ridge brings MAE down to 7.23 h. The relationship between configuration and duration is not well captured by a purely linear model.

Gradient boosting does the heavy lifting. CatBoost and XGBoost land within one standard deviation of each other on every metric. We carry CatBoost forward because it has the lowest mean error across the board.

CatBoost's feature importance concentrates in a handful of features. Model and workload variables are there, as expected, but so are evaluation and checkpoint variables. num_eval_data, the total number of samples processed in evaluation over the life of the job, ranks first, followed by num_trainable_params, batch_eval_size_percent, num_all_params and num_gpus*batch_train_percent, with num_params_saved close behind. SHAP values tell the same story: high evaluation volume consistently pushes the predicted time up, while trainable parameter count has a nonlinear effect that only becomes large for the biggest models.

Mean feature importance of the CatBoost model across folds (PredictionValuesChange).

Feature importance is not a causal claim. Still, the pattern lines up with how a job is actually spent. How often you evaluate, how large the evaluation set is and how many checkpoints you persist all shape the wall-clock time, alongside model and dataset scale. These are choices people often make by habit when writing a training config.

One caveat on reading the plots: larger GPU allocations show positive contributions to predicted time. In our data, bigger allocations go with bigger jobs, so num_gpus acts partly as a proxy for job scale. It does not mean adding GPUs makes a job slower.

Looking at all 272 out-of-fold predictions, the median absolute error is 0.51 h. 80% of predictions are within 2.20 h and 90% are within 4.31 h. Broken down by job duration:

Up to 60 hours, the median error stays at about one hour or less. The long tail is where the model struggles, and with only 13 runs above 60 hours we read that as a data limitation.

Out-of-fold absolute error against real duration, colored by duration range. Errors stay low up to 60 h and widen in the sparse tail.

More data should still help. On a fixed 20% holdout, MAE drops from 1.63 h to 1.50 h when going from 85% to 100% of the training partition, with no clear plateau.

In practice, the predictor gives a scheduler a duration it can plan around before a job starts: which jobs to run together, when GPUs will free up, and whether a run will finish in time for its deadline.

What does transfer is the method: overhead-aware features, multicollinearity-aware selection and the comparison protocol. The full paper will report the feature formulas, the selection procedure, the search spaces and the final hyperparameters in full, so the pipeline can be rebuilt on any execution-log dataset with a similar structure.

Next on our list: hardware-specific descriptors and validation on other GPU types, transfer learning for configurations with few observations, other training objectives, and more data on long runs.

The full paper is coming to arXiv soon. Follow the Dharma-AI organization on Hugging Face to be notified when it is out, and leave your questions in the comments below. We would like to hear how you estimate job duration on your own clusters.

Gladstone Capital (GLAD) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Investment income climbed, but markdowns pulled net asset value lower.

By Motley Fool Transcribing – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:53PM EST

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Gladstone Capital (GLAD -1.96%) reported fiscal year 2026 results highlighted by an 11.7% increase in total investment income and a strategic focus on the lower middle market precision manufacturing sector. Management reported that net investment income remained flat year over year at $2.00 per share as growth in average earning assets was offset by increased financing costs and higher management fees. The company reported a decline in net asset value to $20.77 per share, primarily driven by unrealized depreciation in nonaccrual assets and specific equity positions. Strategic initiatives for the coming fiscal year include returning nonearning investments to earning status and managing leverage within a target range of 1.1 times net asset value.

David Gladstone: Thank you. This is David Gladstone, and this is year-end call. We've had good years and good quarters. So everything seems to be fun at the company. Thank you for calling in. Joining me this morning are David Gladstone, Chairman, Bob Marcotte, our Chief Executive Officer; Mike McQuigg, our President; and Nicole Schaltenbrand, our Chief Financial Officer. We're always happy to talk with our shareholders and analysts and to provide an update on the company. I'll begin with a brief disclosure regarding certain regulatory matters. Today's call may include forward-looking statements, which are based on management's estimates, assumptions and projections.

There are no guarantees of future performance, and actual results may differ materially from those expressed or implied in these statements due to various uncertainties, including the risk factors set forth in our SEC filings, which you can find on the Investors page of our website, gladstonecapital.com. We assume no obligation to update any of these statements unless required by law. Please visit our website for a copy of our Form 10-K, earnings press release and investor presentation for more detailed information. Bob and Nicole will refer to a few slides from the presentation this morning. You can also sign up for our e-mail notification service and find information on how to contact our Investor Relations department.

Now I'll turn the call over to Gladstone Capital's Chief Executive Officer, Bob Marcotte.

Robert Marcotte: Thank you, Catherine, and good morning, and thank you all for dialing in. Mike McQuigg, who joined Gladstone in 2015 and became President of Gladstone Capital on October 1, is with us this morning, and we'll join Nicole and I for your questions. I'll cover the highlights for the quarter and the fiscal year, how we invest and cover some of the valuation movements in the portfolio. Nicole will provide some details on the financial results, and I'll conclude with the market, our investment activity and our near-term outlook. Beginning with last quarter's results. Net interest income -- net investment income came in at $11 million or $0.49 per share for the period, in line with the prior quarter.

Investment balances and yields held and our net interest margin was 8.2%. Fee and dividend income of $200,000 was below historical averages, and with a $480,000 incentive fee credit from our adviser, net investment income after preferred dividends covered 100% of our $0.45 regular monthly distributions. For the year, total investment income rose 11.7%, and net investment income of $2 per share covered our regular distributions at 104.5% after preferred dividends. The net asset value declined $0.73 to $20.77 per share on the quarter, $0.18 of which was -- of the decline was the supplemental distribution we paid in September. And the balance from unrealized depreciation of the portfolio, net of a small realized gain.

About 2/3 of the depreciation for the quarter was on loans to 2 existing nonaccrual investments, which had the effect of reducing our nonearning assets to 2.8% of the portfolio fair value. The depreciation of several appreciated equity investments also contributed to the unrealized depreciation for the period, which is not uncommon, as small moves in EBITDA or market multiples can impact our equity positions. I'll get into some of the valuation movements in a few moments. However, I think it's a good time to reiterate the underlying discipline of our investment focus, process and structuring, which has not fundamentally changed. We have been lending to the lower middle market for 25 years.

While the companies we lend to can grow to be relatively large, we do not compete in the broader middle market loan arena. Our borrowers are established growth-oriented businesses with EBITDA of typically under $10 million in origination, and most of them are backed by private equity sponsors. We find the competitive dynamics in the lower middle market to be attractive, as our sources suggest about 80% of buyout transactions are under $100 million. Conversely, most of the private credit inflows are increasingly centered upmarket, as indicated by the 79% of direct lending funds raised in 2026 went to funds over $5 billion according to PitchBook.

And a fund that size is certainly not going to come down to compete with us. So while we operate in the broad deal market, which is outside the strike zone of most of the institutional capital flows, that allows us to be selective and disciplined regarding our credit, secured structure and economic terms. We originate nearly all of our investments ourselves. We look at each one as a business and not as a financial transaction. We do our diligence in much the same manner as an equity investor would, albeit we use the insight to lead the structuring, documentation and act as agent for most of our investments, which include financial maintenance covenants on all our loans.

The teams sourcing and underwriting each investment are responsible for continuing to monitor the investment until it's exited. As a result, we believe we hold very few of the same loans as the other public BDCs, and our credit results depend on our own underwriting and portfolio management. As validation of our approach over the last 10 years on a split-adjusted basis, GLAD's net investment value grew -- per share grew by $3.53, and we paid $18.14 per share in distributions, which is a return of 1.6x the average public BDC over the same period. Turning to how we manage the credit.

We have board observation rights or sit on the board of nearly all of our portfolio companies and receive monthly financials so we can anticipate credit issues and take action sooner. When a business underperforms, we decide what changes might be required and whether capital from us would improve recovery or add to our exposure. Last quarter, that meant advancing capital to 2 of our non-earning companies and electing not to invest further in a third. Net unrealized depreciation came in at $13.6 million for the period. However, the 7 largest decliners accounted for $20.2 million of markdowns, and the rest of the portfolio was up a net $6.6 million.

Delineating the depreciation a bit further, $8.7 million decline was driven by Eegee's and B+T, which have been on nonaccrual. The largest is Eegee's, an Arizona restaurant business we control, and we discussed in the last quarter, revenue was weaker than we anticipated when -- which reduced the value of the business, and we advanced additional capital to support the new management's plans. Unrealized depreciation on the loan was $5.5 million for the quarter. The second is B+T, the telecommunication services company that has been on nonaccrual for more than a year. We elected not to invest further and wrote the remaining $3.2 million of loan value down to 0.

We expect to exit that position in the December quarter with no further impact on our NAV. On the equity side, 3 positions resulted in unrealized depreciation of $7.1 million, which included a dredging contractor, a water well driller in Texas and a health care marketing services business. Earnings at each fell short of plan, but all 3 are performing businesses and 2 of the 3 positions are still valued above our cost. Net of the nonaccrual and equity valuations outlined, the balance of the portfolio continued to perform well with nominal depreciation.

As of the end of the quarter, our nonearning investments were unchanged at 5 companies with a cost basis of $52.9 million or 6% of debt investments at cost and a fair value of $23.8 million or 2.8% of fair value compared with $30.6 million or 3.6% of portfolio at June 30. Workouts take time, and I'm not going to put a date on them, but I can point to our record. Over the last 5 years, our equity investments produced approximately $64.2 million of net gains realized and unrealized, which more than covered the $26.3 million of net losses on our loans. We remain confident in the outlook for these businesses and anticipate their improvement in the coming quarters.

And now I'll turn the call over to Nicole to provide some details of the fund's financial results for the quarter. Nicole?

Nicole Schaltenbrand: Thanks, Bob. Good morning, everyone. During the September quarter, total interest income rose $100,000 to $24.4 million as average earning assets were largely unchanged, while the weighted average yield on our interest-bearing portfolio declined slightly to 11.7% for the period. Total investment income was $24.6 million compared with $24.5 million in the prior quarter. PIK income for the quarter declined to $1.4 million or 5.7% of interest income. Total expenses were largely unchanged at $13.6 million versus the prior quarter as interest expense rose $300,000 with a full quarter of the 7% notes issued in June, net of lower average line of credit borrowings, and net base management fees rose $200,000.

These increases were offset by a $600,000 decline in net incentive fees, which includes a $500,000 incentive fee credit for the quarter. After $900,000 of preferred dividends, it was $10.2 million or 100% of the regular monthly distributions declared on common shares. With respect to net asset value, as laid out on Slide 9 of our investor presentation, we began the quarter at $21.50 per share. Net investment income was $0.49 less $0.04 of preferred dividend, covered our $0.45 of regular distributions.

The supplemental distribution reduced NAV by $0.18, and net unrealized depreciation reduced it by $0.48 on debt investments and $0.13 on equity investments, which is partially offset by $0.06 of realized gains, which brings us to $20.77 as of September 30. The net decrease in net assets resulting from operations was $2.2 million or $0.10 per share as impacted by the unrealized depreciation covered by Bob earlier. For the fiscal year, total investment income rose $10.5 million or 11.7% to $99.6 million as average earning assets rose $121.9 million or 17.9%, and while the weighted average yield declined to 11.9% from 12.7% in the prior year, driven mainly by lower SOFR rates.

Total expenses rose by about the same amount with interest expense up $5 million on higher average line of credit borrowings and the change in mix of our debt. Net management fees were up $2.9 million on higher average and higher average assets and net incentive fees were up $1.9 million as the incentive fee credit was $500,000 this year compared to $2.3 million in the prior year. Net investment income for the year was $45.1 million or $2 per share compared with $45.2 million or $2.02 per share last year. After $2.6 million of preferred dividends, net investment income covered 104.5% of our regular distributions for the year.

With $11.5 million of net unrealized depreciation for the year, the net increase in net assets resulting from operations was $32 million or $1.42 per share. We paid $1.98 per share in regular and supplemental distributions for the year and NAV per share declined $0.57. Moving over to the balance sheet. As of September 30, total assets rose to $973 million, consisting of $956 million in investments at fair value and $17.7 million in cash and other assets. Liabilities were $453 million as of September 30, up from $439 million at June 30, with the increase in total debt.

Borrowings under our $385 million line of credit were $188 million as of the end of the quarter, and the remaining balance of our liabilities consist primarily of $149.5 million of 5.875% convertible notes due in October 2030, $60 million of 7% notes due December 2029 and $50 million of 3.75% notes due in May. Availability under the line of credit was approximately $178 million at the end of the quarter. The line is our only floating rate borrowing at 42% of principal debt, while about 85% of our debt investments carry floating rate. During the quarter, we also issued $6.8 million of our 6.25% Series A preferred stock, which has a principal balance of $57 million.

As of September 30, net assets declined $16.4 million since June 30 to $469.3 million. Debt to equity, including our preferred stock, was 1.08x compared with 1x at June 30. On principal debt alone, it was 0.95x compared to 0.9x at June 30. With respect to distributions, monthly distributions for November and December will be $0.15 per common share which is an annual run rate of $1.80 per share. The Board will meet again in January to determine the monthly distribution to common stockholders for the following quarter. Our accumulated spillover was approximately $5.5 million or $0.24 per share as of September 30. And now I'll turn it back to Bob for the market and our outlook.

Robert Marcotte: Thank you, Nicole. Turning to the market. Deal activity in the larger market swings with the rates in the economy, and it's been flat to slowing for most of the year. The lower middle market is less affected as its deal flow is often driven by family-owned or founder-owned businesses changing hands. As outlined in our investor presentation posted last night, the lower middle market continues to see relatively stable purchase multiples and modest leverage in the range of 2.9 to 3.7x EBITDA over the past 2 years as well as a healthy pricing premium on borrowers with less than $10 million of EBITDA compared to the more competitive middle market.

Based on the modest leverage profile of our investments, we occasionally see smaller commercial banks in the mix, but their structural inflexibility and responsiveness can be an impediment. To the extent that we might see other unitranche competitors for attractive PE-sponsored situations, where appropriate, we've been successful in winning the deals by bringing in senior lenders to blend down the overall loan rate and continuing to agent and manage the credit as we have historically done. Short-term rates moved up 0.25 point in this mid-September time frame. So most of the effect on our earnings starts in the December quarter.

With most of our loans at floating rates and more than half of our borrowings fixed, this increase and any further movement would serve to enhance our net investment income. Turning to our investment activity. We reviewed about 500 opportunities in the fiscal year just ended and closed 19 transactions. We invested $309 million for the year, including $171 million in 11 new portfolio companies. With repayments down to $210 million from $352 million in the prior year, the portfolio grew by $97 million. Fundings last quarter totaled $84 million and included 2 new investments totaling $33 million and $51 million on the quarter were additional advances to existing portfolio companies.

Both of the new investments are precision manufacturing businesses where order backlogs have been strong. In the aggregate, our originations last quarter had an average leverage of 3.2x EBITDA, 48% loan-to-value at entry and a spread of 730 basis points over SOFR. Exits and repayments came in at $71 million, so net originations were $13 million in the quarter. The 2 exits were legacy energy-related investments that we had anticipated. With respect to the portfolio, first lien debt and total debt investments came in at 74% and 90% of the portfolio cost.

As our leverage has moved up, we've become more judicious about where we are allocating new capital and prioritizing strongly performing portfolio companies that benefit from the scale associated with additional capacity or acquisitions. Since the end of the quarter, we funded $6.9 million in an existing portfolio company to fund an attractive acquisition, and we'll continue to manage our pipeline of late-stage deals generally in line with the near-term repayments and liquidity events. In reflecting on our recently concluded fiscal '26 and the outlook for the next quarter or 2, I'd like to leave you with the following: Total investment income and portfolio each grew more than 11% this year.

But with lower rates, higher financing costs and higher net management fees, net investment income was about flat. We are working to return our non-earning investments to earning status or recover and deploy this capital to scale our earnings as we manage our balance sheet leverage in the range of 1.1x NAV. We reset the regular distribution to $0.15 a month in October 2025 and net investment income after preferred dividends has covered each quarter since.

Going forward, we expect to continue to support the dividend coverage through a combination of fee and dividend income, monetizing equity assets to reinvest in earning assets, the recovery of non-earning loans and we'll consider incentive fee waivers as the manager has done in the past. With the assets we added this year and the recent increase in short-term rates, which equates to about $0.06 a share in NII lift, we expect to continue to sustain our coverage. We are an investment-driven culture, and our priority for fiscal '27 is to improve our net investment income per share while continuing to deliver top quarter economic returns to our shareholders, which they have enjoyed for the past decade or more.

So I'd be really interested in understanding what your thesis is on deal flow for next year.

Robert Marcotte: Mickey, there are obviously mixed signals. I say that the forward curve for SOFR is going up. That's not a good thing for buyouts. There's certainly been some measure of volatility around economic conditions and tariffs and the like. That said, I think the recent political events have created a scenario where there's more likely to be a roadblock or a do-nothing environment from a regulatory perspective. So I do think that some of these portfolio companies have gotten long in the tooth and it's time to sell and the LP pressure on the PE funds is pretty significant. When we look at the sale transactions, we really look at both the refinancing transactions and sales transactions.

In the refinancing environment, a large number of our companies have really grown. The clear appreciation of their multiples in the market has changed, and these tend to be growth-oriented businesses. So what we see in a more challenged environment are companies that have grown, that have delivered, that have good growth outlook are going to continue to trade. So that's kind of one. Two, based on the scale, the market opportunities are there for them to refinance. Obviously, we tend to hold to our starting terms when we originate assets.

And if EBITDA is 2 or 3 or 4x what we entered at, there's plenty of folks upmarket that will leverage that at a lower rate or at a higher multiple. I do think there are a number of plays in our business and sectors where growth rate will just dictate acquisition and larger sponsors are going to want to get into that business. They're seeing huge amounts of defense and aerospace spend and contracts that are going to grow dramatically and consolidating to become a larger and more encompassed and capable competitor in that business is critical to the primes to continue to supply the growth in some of those end markets. So we would expect the activity to be somewhat tied to certain growth sectors. We're seeing that in some of our businesses even at the scale that we are, and we would expect that to continue.

I don't think the financial arbitrage, the buying at a high level of leverage at an elevated multiple is a game that is going to be particularly successful in 2027, but that's not where we see the M&A market. Our companies start at roughly 7 to 7.5 turns on a multiple basis. When they grow to $20 million or $30 million of EBITDA, they will trade for 9x, 12x depending on the underlying industry. So the core drivers to the sale transactions for our business are very different than the broader upmarket environment, which I think is going to continue to be challenged by the flow of funds.

The net redemptions in the -- in the private BDCs is certainly not creating a particularly robust environment for financing in that sector. So bottom line is I think the lower middle market is going to continue to trade. That's where most of the transactions happen. And I feel pretty good about where our companies are in seeing that liquidity. And as I said, we'll continue to monitor that and driving our investment activities going forward.

Mickey Schleien: And Bob, if I could follow up with one question on what you just said. I mean your new originations came in at attractive spreads during the quarter that you just reported. But you've also talked about repayment and refinancing activity as your portfolio companies grow. Does that pose too much of a headwind? Or do you see opportunities to stabilize or perhaps even improve your portfolio yields, notwithstanding what the forward curve -- I don't know what the interest rates are going to be a year from now. The forward curve is notoriously wrong. Just big picture, what are you thinking about portfolio yields?

Robert Marcotte: Mickey, it's an interesting question. I would say I think about it 2 ways. The refinancing activity, yes, I don't believe that people are going to be able to dramatically reduce their cost of funds, but that's not the only reason people would consider refinancings. They might be coming to the end of the maturity, they need to extend. They might take a lesser upfront -- a rate that is probably not as attractive, but it's cheaper than where we are. And they might look for a distribution so they could share that money with their LPs in order to provide the liquidity that they're screaming for given the age of some of the companies in these portfolios.

So the refinancing activity may be more leverage driven than rate driven as part of their motivation. As it relates to our improvement of our portfolio yields, one thing that we do not do is we do not do financial transactions in and of themselves. We are not an opportunistic lender. We are not looking for turnaround situations. We are not looking to juice our yields.

The fact that we are putting our investments out in low 3s on a senior secured basis and delivering spreads in the low 7s, I wouldn't want to push that too much because we are going to be taking on credit quality, and we may be moving down the capital stack and increase our risk. So overall, I would say we're probably at the top end of our yield. The only thing that we might do is maybe we do more first out facilities, we're bringing a bank in. We can get a little bit of better pricing off the back end of those situations.

But I wouldn't want to chase yield to materially increase our leverage or credit risk profile, which is core to our business. The last piece I would say is we really like attractive growth businesses. I'd rather finance at an attractive rate, a growth-oriented business than stretch yield because in many cases, we will invest in the equity of that company, too, and we will get a lift on the equity side. As my comments said, we've been very successful in realizing gains on our equity, and we've got a number that we are hoping in 2027 will come to fruition. So good companies also bring equity upside for our co-investments.

And I'd rather do that than chase a turnaround situation where that might not be as clear.

Operator: Our next question is from Erik Zwick with Lucid Capital Markets.

Erik Zwick: I wanted to -- a bit of a maybe a follow-up from Mickey's question just with the outlook currently, the futures curve would suggest that interest rates are going to go higher. We'll see if they do or not. But just curious if you could update us as of kind of, I guess, November -- sorry, September 30, what is the average and the range of debt service coverage ratios for your portfolio companies today?

Robert Marcotte: The average -- weighted average leverage in the portfolio today is 3.7x and the weighted average fixed charge coverage is 2.5x.

Erik Zwick: Can you guys still hear me? Sorry, I'm traveling today, having some reception issues.

Erik Zwick: Okay. Great. I guess the only other question I had today, just looking at some of the fair value changes, and thanks for the ones that you addressed in the prepared comments. Curious if you had any commentary you could add on Dutch Gold Honey, which saw a nice appreciation in the quarter.

Robert Marcotte: Dutch Gold, there are some people that would ask questions about consumer trends. Dutch Gold is one of the largest distributors of honey in the Eastern United States. It was bought at a very fairly low multiple. The business did not fall off as much as was expected. In fact, it went the other way, and it's continued to increase as part of the transition from the founding family that have been owning the business for 75 years. They had not been investing in the plant. The sponsor that came in that we assisted in the financing pulled -- laid out a very detailed capital expansion plan to increase their capacity and grow their customer base.

They have executed on that and beginning to pull in additional revenues. So not only did it not go down, but it's dramatically expanding. And the multiples in food and food service-related businesses, particularly around healthy, attractive natural ingredients like honey, which is being used increasingly as a substitute in a variety of ways, it continues to grow. So low multiple expansion, new customers, and growth and exit multiple appreciation are all combining to a very attractive framework here. I suspect there's probably still more upside in that particular business.

And I also suspect that, that may be one on the horizon that the sponsor would consider selling since they've largely recouped all of their equity and are still very much in the black.

Operator: Our next question is from Christopher Nolan with Ladenburg Thalmann.

Christopher Nolan: Bob, thanks for your comments on the current sponsor environment. What industry sectors are you seeing that have the best organic growth? And the general gist of the question is since a financial arbitrage is really not an option for many of these sponsors, they have to grow their portfolio companies for an exit. So just trying to see what industry sectors are you seeing the most growth?

Robert Marcotte: Well, the easy one on that one is going to be anything that's downstream of data centers or fab construction. I mean it's dominating the economic environment. The concern is that those at some point, will slow. Backlog is very rich. And I think through '27, it's very clear that those are growing. Derivative off that are probably things that are defense or defense-related. And then lastly, I would say, electrical infrastructure grid-type investments. I mean I'll give you a couple of points. Viron in the portfolio is a business that does coated stainless steel tubing and products that go into fab construction. Their backlog is probably approaching more than a year.

Their growth rate has more than doubled in the last year. I think they've got a good backlog, but that's a scenario where that could either refinance or could sell their business. We certainly have a number of precision metal fabrication businesses that are supplying some of the industries I've alluded to. The missile space, aerospace backlogs continue to grow, whether it's gas turbines or whether it's aircraft turbines. That market is growing dramatically, and we have probably 4 or 5 investments that are otherwise tied to that particular industry. Backlogs -- the businesses have grown, backlogs are as strong as they've ever been, and their customers are looking for more capacity to feed into that market.

So we are seeing kind of a multiple of plays that are around precision manufacturing, domestic production. And what I would say is there is an increased level of sophistication that the large end customers are looking for, whether it's production capacity, whether it's automation, whether it's new machinery production capabilities, whether it's specifications and detailed performance specs, all of those things take investment. And between the capital base and the sponsors that we finance and their forward vision for those businesses, what we're finding is the private equity improvements in these underlying businesses are distancing themselves from what is a very broad base of industrial small-scale family-run businesses.

If you're in aerospace, if you're a private jet manufacturer as an example, and you've got 300 suppliers, there's a small smattering of those companies that have the people, the knowledge base, the wherewithal to continue to expand and improve their production capabilities. So last quarter, one of our investments happened to be the #1 rated supplier to a private jet manufacturer that has been supplying this company for 50 years. And by virtue of our investment and some additional capital going in, they are going to give us more business because we -- the company has an unblemished track record of delivering.

So there is an element that's going on today in these growth scenarios with the right ingredients, there is nice growth off of the entry multiples for these businesses that consistently trade in the 7, 8x range. And between growth and that multiple arbitrage, those investors are going to see very nice returns.

Christopher Nolan: For the industries which are not growing, do you foresee that the private equity is just going to sort of try to extend until better days? Or how do you see that happening?

Robert Marcotte: The zombies, I think what we're seeing obviously, there are some businesses that are more challenged. The way we look at those businesses, we see most of them coming to us for refinancings. They've been in the game for 4 or 5 years or maybe they were done in 2020 or 2021. And now they need to refinance the debt because it's coming to the end, and they haven't necessarily grown their business. We generally pass on those situations in the refinancing market. If they're in our portfolio in that context, typically, what we're seeing is they don't have the growth. They don't need to spend on new capacity. They don't have the working capital demands from an expanding business.

They should be able to generate free cash flow because our average portfolio has relatively low leverage. And at some point, the leverage gets to a point of saying, are we the right lender for a business that's got a low growth? If you're 2.5 turns of leverage, if you're 2 turns of leverage and where average portfolio is SOFR 7, why don't you go to a bank and borrow for SOFR 3.5 and you can net the difference in increased distributions to the equity. So we tend to look at those situations as they deleverage. And naturally, they will go to the bank, and we won't chase them because it doesn't make any sense.

We are not the right partner for a non-growth business that's organically deleveraging.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Our next question is from Robert Dodd with Raymond James.

Robert Dodd: On the topic -- I mean, you brought up -- there's been some political changes recently. I mean, for founder-owned businesses, well, for the sponsor-owned businesses as well, obviously, tax planning is often a factor in motivating a sale decision action, whatever it is. And while I don't think there's going to be any tax changes imminently, if the political shift continues, I got to think the worries of tax changes in maybe beyond '28 increase. Do you think that's room to accelerate anything, particularly maybe in your founder-owned business target segment, maybe not what's in your portfolio already, obviously, but the opportunities.

I mean, do you think you could see an accelerated cycle because of those kind of concerns over the next couple of years?

Robert Marcotte: It's been a while since taxes have driven transactions. I would say, it used to be a more meaningful impact. Most of the founders that we deal with probably in separating themselves and stepping away from a business they might have worked for 30 or 40 years and in many cases, well into their 70s. Taxes is probably not the biggest concern. It might be the reason they start the conversation, but that's not why they're selling the business. I do think if there is impetus to some measure of tax increases, it might make some sense that people think about it. But the fundamentals of why they're selling are far more material.

I mean, we've looked at transactions where the owner's in his early '90s and had debated selling the business. And it really wasn't tax. It was the family issues. It was the ownership, it was the self-perspective. So I think tax may, at the margin, increase it somewhat, but family-oriented businesses are not going to be driven by those dynamics as much. They either have a successor. They either have a management team that they can continue to run. They may or may not have the capital base necessary to grow their business and continue to compete in the marketplace, as I've described. All of those things would tend to be more significant drivers, and I don't see that changing.

Robert Dodd: Got it. Got it. And then one not really a follow-up. On the first-out type things where you've been bringing banks in, has that market gotten more competitive? Or I mean, if it's more competitive and the banks are willing to do more, that's good for you, right? But how -- are you happy with the terms, the structure, how that market -- how your use of that market has evolved a little bit because obviously, it does have some -- to your point, there's some advantages on the return to you and the lower cost of overall debt to the borrower maybe.

Robert Marcotte: It's constantly changing, Robert. I would say there's always a smattering of midsized or regional banks who don't have the origination capabilities to put on C&I loans on their own, right? And so teaming up with an experienced creditor that has responsible management and does the diligence on their companies is one way for them to put on C&I loans. And those folks, once they see the way we approach our deals and underwrite our deals and provide diligence information, they're frankly clamoring to see more transactions from us. But at the same time, those businesses can be acquired or they can be merged or they could ultimately develop their own origination capabilities.

And so they will run it for a period of time and maybe they will transition out. There has generally been a positive movement of more folks available in that marketplace. So I don't feel we're constrained. But it's a kind of a constant turnover. The general rates are there. What we bring them in is relatively low leverage compared to what they would see on a straight-up basis. And frankly, they don't have the ability to access the buyout market and the private equity sponsors in quite the same way. So our ability to blend them into a solution that works for the sponsors, they don't have the knowledge, the experience or the structuring capabilities.

So they are very reliant on us to set them up and get them in the deals. I expect that opportunity to continue. We generally only do that for deals that we feel very strongly about the performance and the growth, want to be more competitive. And we typically only bring them in for a very modest amount of leverage. Those deals typically are 2 plus or minus turns of leverage. So it's relatively risk-free. And if there's ever an issue, we feel we can always either pay them out or refinance them out as the case may be.

So it's a market that I see positive growth in, but it will always have turnovers, and you always have large-scale banks that won't do those transactions. So you're dealing with something in the smaller category or specialized lenders that will participate in the transactions with us.

Operator: We have reached the end of our question-and-answer session. I would like to turn the conference back over to Bob Marcotte for closing remarks.

Robert Marcotte: Thank you all for your questions today and for calling in. We look forward to updating you on the next quarter. This concludes our call. Thank you very much.

Operator: Thank you. This will conclude today's conference. You may disconnect at this time, and thank you for your participation.

Last week the Apple HomeView arrived in users’ homes and immediately required a software update to version 27.2.

Nvidia said its AI data center loan guarantees won't kick in until facilities are built

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

The company's maximum gross exposure on guarantees totaled $118 billion as of Oct. 25, and it has made no payments under them

Nvidia $NVDA said on Tuesday that its guarantees for data center buildouts take effect phase by phase, once each related building is completed and ready for service.

The largest guarantee, entered into in August, provides credit support on the land, power and shell buildout for about 4.25 gigawatts across nine buildings at SB Energy's PORTS-Pike Technology Campus in Ohio, the company said. The campus will host Nvidia infrastructure under 20-year leases to OpenAI, subject to limited exceptions.

The first guarantee is expected to take effect in fiscal 2029, Nvidia said. Its exposure builds as each building comes online, up to a maximum of $105 billion, then declines over each 20-year lease term as OpenAI makes lease payments. Nvidia has the option to provide credit support in phases for about 3.8 additional gigawatts as the site scales.

Maximum gross exposure on all of Nvidia's guarantees totaled $118 billion as of Oct. 25. That figure includes $105 billion in SB Energy credit support guarantees, $9.5 billion in power and systems credit support guarantees and $3.5 billion in land, power and shell guarantees for neoclouds. Nvidia said it has made no payments under its guarantees.

Nvidia said separate commitments to neoclouds take effect once the neoclouds secure financing to buy the data center infrastructure and the related compute is deployed, made operational and accepted by Nvidia. If those conditions are not resolved on time, certain commitments could be reduced or eliminated, the company said.

Interim CEO and CFO Colette Kress told analysts on Tuesday's earnings call that a guarantee ends if a customer is delayed or runs into trouble building, according to TechCrunch. "Our guarantee doesn't exist anymore," she said. Asked whether the terms were new, Kress said they simply spelled out conditions that already applied and did not signal a shift in direction.

Kress said Nvidia will treat help with land, power and shell for data centers as a top priority while reviewing which types of companies it will help finance, according to TechCrunch.

Nvidia reported fiscal third-quarter revenue of $115.8 billion, up 103% from a year earlier, on Tuesday. The results were its first since co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week after admitting to an affair with a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed, a major Nvidia customer. Kress took over as interim CEO while keeping her CFO role, and the board began a search for a permanent chief executive.

Should You Buy Walmart Stock Before Nov. 19? History Has a Clear Answer.

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Walmart's earnings days are a coin flip, but its long-term record tells a much clearer story.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 18, 2026 at 1:51PM EST

Pop quiz: What did Walmart (WMT -0.16%) stock do the last four times the company reported earnings?

If you guessed "went up, because it's Walmart," you're one for four. Shares jumped about 6.5% after the November 2025 report, slipped about 1.4% in February, dropped about 7% in May, and tumbled about 9% in August.

Walmart reports again before the opening bell on Thursday, Nov. 19, with results due around 7 a.m. ET and a call with management an hour later. So should you buy before then? History gives a pretty clear answer -- just not the one you might expect.

Those four reactions had more to do with the forecast than the quarter. In May, sales beat expectations, but cautious guidance amid soaring gas prices sent shares lower. In August, Walmart raised its full-year outlook and still fell as Wall Street fixated on weaker-than-expected U.S. same-store sales. Walmart tends to lowball that forecast, too. After February's soft outlook, Baird analyst Peter Benedict pointed to the company's "history of conservatism."

Thursday's bar is high. Shares trade at about 46 times earnings and sit near their 52-week high after climbing about 7% in the past month. That's a rich P/E ratio, and CFO John David Rainey noted in August that Walmart was absorbing more than $2 billion in extra fuel costs "in arguably a softer consumer environment."

Zoom out, though, and those scary drops shrink fast. Someone who bought two days before August's plunge is still ahead today, and shares are up about 26% from a week before last November's report.

The longer view says the same thing. In its latest annual report, Walmart showed that $100 invested in February 2021, with dividends reinvested, grew to about $272 by early 2026, versus about $201 for the S&P 500. The interesting part is that the first two years were dull, with that $100 worth only about $106 by early 2023. That's the trouble with market timing. The payoff often shows up while you're not looking.

Walmart has also raised its dividend for 53 straight years. This move of over 50 straight years of raises makes it a Dividend King.

What keeps me interested is what Walmart actually does in the 90-odd days between earnings reports. Furner, a three-decade Walmart veteran, took over as CEO on Feb. 1, and he's betting on speed. "Speed isn't simply a fulfillment metric; it's an acquisition strategy," he said in August, adding that fast-delivery customers shop more often and are more likely to join Walmart+.

The newest piece arrives by air. On Nov. 5, Walmart agreed to add 300 Zipline drone delivery sites nationwide. "We see customers try drone delivery for the novelty and then continue to come back to it for the convenience," said Greg Cathey, who leads Walmart's e-commerce fulfillment transformation.

Walmart is also quietly making money on a lot more than groceries. Its advertising business, which includes Walmart Connect and Vizio, and its memberships both continued to grow last quarter. "The math isn't simply one plus one equals two," Furner said of how those businesses feed each other.

Then there's artificial intelligence. Walmart already sells through ChatGPT, Google's Gemini, and others, and it's rolling out Agent Profiles that let shoppers set a spending limit for an AI agent. "We price products, not people," the company wrote, promising agents see the same prices humans do. This holiday season, its Sparky assistant can even make an item's shelf tag light up in the store so you can find it.

So, should you buy Walmart stock before Nov. 19? History says the date barely matters. If you're buying just to catch a pop, the last four reports say you're flipping a coin. If you want to own Walmart for the next decade, buy when you're ready, maybe in pieces, so a rough Thursday becomes a chance to add more instead of a reason to panic.

Why I’m Walking Away From Nursing

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

​I’m Cat. I work as a registered nurse, and starting in January, I’ll be trading in my scrubs for trail runners and a backpack. My husband and I are known to be spontaneous and challenge ourselves when life starts feeling boring. Packing a U-Haul with whatever fits and moving to a state we’ve never been to, hiking to the top of a mountain in Colorado to exchange vows, buying a house 14 hours away we’ve only seen pictures of online; you could say we live for the spontaneity of life. My motivation for hiking the trail goes far beyond last-minute decision-making; it comes from more recent experiences while working as a nurse.

Exchanging vows on the summit of Mount Bierstadt (14,065 feet)

​Nursing has this way of showing you just how fragile life really is. Having worked as a float tech in all units of the hospital and recently as a nurse in ICU step-down and rehab, I’ve cared for adults, both young and old, who lost their health and independence in an instant. I quickly realized I’ve been taking my own health for granted. I didn’t quite know I was in search of a challenge in life, but as always, my husband was at the driver’s seat of a new adventure. This time, it was all on me.

​It all started sitting by the fire in our backyard. My seasoned thru-hiker husband asked if I wanted to hike the Appalachian Trail; I shrugged and said okay. A week later, he suggested adding the Florida Trail first to get my trail legs ready; I quickly agreed to that, too. By the third week, he suggested I hike the entire ECT. Just like that, a casual backyard fire turned into a 4,500-mile hike.

​I’ll be hiking the Eastern Continental Trail (ECT), which is a connection of trails spanning from Florida to Canada. I decided to blog my adventures to have something to look back on and to share my travels with friends and family. I don’t know what lies ahead of me, but what I do know is I’m about to make so many memories, connections, and mistakes!

If you’d like to follow along with the journey, I’ll be sharing updates, photos, and stories right here while I head north.

​The very basic core of a man’s living spirit is his passion for adventure. The joy of life comes from our encounters with new experiences, and hence there is no greater joy than to have an endlessly changing horizon, for each day to have a new and different sun.

—Chris McCandless (​Alexander Supertramp)

Hey, I'm Cat! I've been working as a registered nurse for the last couple of years, but before that, I was a pastry chef turned correctional officer turned dental assistant. You could say I embrace change and enjoy a challenge! My husband and I lived in Colorado for six years, which is where I found my true love for the outdoors. GTA 6 Has Flock-Like Cameras To Track Your Movements

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

If you thought you were going to escape the nightmare of our growing surveillance state by blowing off some steam in the sprawling world of "Grand Theft Auto 6" this week, I've got some bad news for you. Just like if you actually boosted a car on the streets of Miami, there are cameras and police databases to keep an eye on your every simulated illegal movement. Just having the fastest car on the map isn't going to cut it anymore. To successfully make Vice City their domain, players are going to have to think strategically.

Video leaked in August showed the main character speeding by traffic cameras. The heads-up display showed three connected dots to indicate the car was being targeted by a license plate scanning camera, according to PCGamer. Passing those cameras will alert the cops to your location, and the you'll immediately enter a red zone where the authorities are hunting for you.

If you keep driving along the route where you alerted the cameras, the authorities will keep following you, just like in real life. The best way to escape those searching eyes of the flock-like cameras (and therefore the cops) is to low-key drive out of the red zone, just like in previous iterations of the game. Cops will also respond in more force (aka you'll gain more stars) if they know for sure your character is the perp they're seeking. Changing clothes, cars and weapons after a heist can help you give the cops the slip, says Techradar.

It's fair to say that the Jalopnik crew is wildly excited to tear through Vice City on some vaguely familiar but, for licensing purposes, completely unique high-end sports cars and grow our own individual empires. Whatever keeps us inside and away from the watchful eye of our tech overlords. When you emerge from your self-imposed video game exile, rubbing your eyes in the full light of day, just know you didn't waste a week of your life. You've actually just helped make real world driving safer.

The Gifts Chefs and Bartender Actually Want This Year

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Streetwear cooking shoes, a perfect pepper mill, and more

If you buy something from a link, Vox Media may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

This is not your average “gifts for foodies” round-up. We know that you’ve probably been given a few too many wine glasses and cookbooks. “As a professional, I don’t want to be gifted introductory-level items that will ultimately go unused,” says Storm Brunni, private dining director at Barolo Grill in Denver. That’s why, for our guide, we’ve consulted you, the hospitality pros, about what you actually would like to receive this year.

New shoes for service. “I’m getting all of my favorite people the new high-top kitchen shoes by Chuck Taylor in leather anti-slip. They’re cool enough to complete your streetwear look, functional enough to wear in the dining room, kitchen, or work, and comfy enough to wear as a commuter.” —Jonathon Sawyer, chef at Kindling in Chicago

An industry-favorite hand cream. “Between constant handwashing, citrus, chiles, and hot pans, hospitality hands take a beating. Add a heated neck massager to use on the couch after a midnight close, and you’ve basically given someone a spa day.” —Antelmo Ambrosio, executive chef at Casa Oaxaca in New York City

Something for the coffee nerds. “It’s definitely a splurge, but I buy myself the Onyx Coffee Advent Calendar every year. A lot of hospitality pros nerd out about coffee, and Onyx is one of the best roasters. They fill the calendar with individual portions of beans (enough for two people) with different origins, roasts, and processes every day. Everything is roasted on a schedule so that when you’re brewing each one, the coffee is at its peak that day. The calendar has some of their signature coffees, alongside one-offs that you’ll never be able to get again, and I’ve tried some of the best coffees of my life in past calendars.” —Katie Renshaw, owner of Bar Bambi in Chicago

An upgrade for kitchen organization. “I love this automatic tape dispenser. Gift it with a nice fresh box of Sharpies.” —Kevin Beary, beverage director of Gus’ Sip & Dip, Kitty’s Cosmopolitan Club, Three Dots and a Dash in Chicago

The gift of longer-lasting produce. “Vejibag is a family-owned business, run by a small team of women who create 100 percent organic cotton bags designed to keep produce fresher for longer at home. They genuinely work and are an amazing addition to any kitchen.” —Lauren DeSteno, executive chef at Ai Fiori in New York City

A tool for operators who know there’s always something that needs fixing. “I was given a precision screwdriver from HOTO Tools and now I’m obsessed with the brand and their design aesthetic. So many things in the restaurants need little fixes all the time and the 3.6V Electric Screwdriver Kit Pro has all the bits you could need. I also love their rotary drill for polishing plateware with chips/scratches for work.” —Zach Engel, chef at Galit and Cafe Yaya in Chicago

Gifts with a personal touch. “As a general manager some of the top staff requests I’ve received are for nice wine keys (it’s an even nicer touch if they’re engraved) and custom server books.” —John Schneekloth, general manager at Toro in Chicago

Nice dishware is worthwhile at home, too. “I’d love to receive items from Ivo Angel, a beautiful ceramics company out of Italy. I am a huge fan of the splatter design and always want a pinch bowl; I can’t have too many. Also the olive bowl with an attached vessel for pits is perfect for hosting.” —Julia Jaksoc, chef and owner of Bar Roza, Cafe Roze and Roze Pony in Nashville

The pepper mill that chefs swear by. “It’s one of those tools that gets used constantly, makes a real difference, and is something every cook or bartender would appreciate having.” —Marco Torres, Chef at Brass and The Tusk Bar in New York City

Fun socks. “Being locked down in heavy black pants, unforgiving non-slip shoe leathers, and a stiff chef coat means you have to find your personality somewhere. A pair of loud, ridiculous socks underneath always makes me feel a little more human. I’ll never forget getting this pair of bright yellow Spam socks as a Christmas gift one year. Honestly, it’s one of the best presents I’ve ever unwrapped, and it brings a little joy each time I slip them on.” —Soo Ahn, chef/partner at Adalina Prime, Arla and Adalina Italian in Chicago

Something to take the edge off. “For the industry pro who enjoys a little puff puff and a good nap (especially after a Saturday morning shift with lines out the door), you can’t go wrong with Tokin’ Jew rolling papers. We sell them at the deli because, come on, but they’re also a fantastic gift. And for the pipe people, their pickle pipes are another way to keep it kosher.” —Jake Schneider, chef and co-owner of Schneider Deli in Chicago

A beautiful book for recipe writing. “I would gift this brown leather journal cover from Shinola along with this 12-pack of pens. I think the notebook is a worthwhile splurge, and while I know the idea of a more expensive and elegant pen is both compelling and sexy, it is also impractical for when you’re on the go or as prone to losing nice pens as I am. The kitchen fit of your dreams. “I could wear this jumpsuit 24/7! Ondo to launch tokenized Anthropic stock on IPO day

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

Eligible investors outside the U.S. can mint and redeem Ondo’s Anthropic stock token around the clock, including on weekends.

Ondo Finance, a crypto company that tokenizes stocks and other assets, plans to launch a token tied to Anthropic shares on Thursday, when the AI company is slated to start trading on Nasdaq.

The token is called ANTon and will be available to eligible investors outside the U.S., according to a Wednesday release.

Anthropic is expected to set its final IPO price after market close on Wednesday before shares debut under the ticker ANT.

The public offering could become the largest in history, with analysts projecting the debut to bring in around $90 billion at the top end of its range. This would edge out the $85.7 billion raised by SpaceX in its record IPO in June.

"ANTon is live on IPO day, it mints and redeems after New York goes home, and it works like other assets onchain," said Ian De Bode, Ondo Finance’s president and acting CEO.

Ondo affirmed that minting and redemption will run 24/7 and said it will waive minting fees and charge a reduced redemption fee. Ondo did not say how long this would apply. The Block reached out to Ondo for clarification.

ANTon gives holders exposure to ANT share prices, but holders will not have direct ownership of the stock. It will join more than 450 other tokenized stocks and ETFs on Ondo Stocks, which the company says has passed $1 billion in total value locked.

Oklahoma’s governor sees universities as ‘stuck in their ways.’ His fix, a 3-year bachelor’s degree, is already spreading fast

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Oklahoma Gov. Kevin Stitt has a problem with the four-year degree, and he brought it to a room full of the people who hire its graduates.

“The academic community are kind of stuck in their ways. They don’t like to change,” Stitt said Monday at the Fortune 500 Innovation Forum in Detroit, on a panel with U.S. Steel CEO David Burritt. He said when he digs into the degree programs at the University of Oklahoma and Oklahoma State, “there’s a book this thick and then you’ve got like three people graduating from these different things.”

He added: “I’m not saying that degree is not important, but you can’t be all things [to] all people.”

His fix is a 90-credit bachelor’s degree, 30 credits short of the standard 120, or roughly a year less of tuition. In-state tuition and fees at public four-year colleges averaged $11,950 in the 2024-25 school year, according to the College Board. Stitt said the idea is to keep the rigor of the requirements, trim some electives, and “get kids ready for the workforce quicker.”

RAND counted 119 reduced-credit bachelor’s programs publicly announced across the U.S. as of May 2026, up from five in 2024, most of them in “applied and workforce-facing fields.” Jason Lee, a senior policy researcher at RAND, has since told the Wall Street Journal the number of shortened bachelor’s programs announced this year are up to roughly 150.

Stitt signed an executive order in February ordering a 90-day study of 90-credit “accelerated” degrees, and the Legislature followed with HB 3315, which required the State Regents to study feasibility. In June, the regents took action to create a framework for the degrees.

“A three-year degree speeds up workforce entry and makes so much sense for students who are ready to work and support a family, right here in Oklahoma,” Stitt said in a statement at the time.

On Sept. 3, the regents approved four reduced-credit degrees ranging from 90 to 103 credit hours, according to the Oklahoma Council of Public Affairs: social work at the University of Oklahoma, two at Oklahoma State, in artificial intelligence and in business, and workforce development at Rogers State University.

“A college degree doesn’t have to take four years just because it always has,” Stitt posted on X.

Other states are moving, too. Tennessee’s higher education commission approved a policy in July allowing bachelor’s degrees of as few as 90 credit hours. Texas Gov. Greg Abbott directed the state’s coordinating board to develop pathways in August. The University of North Carolina system approved a pilot in April, and 11 campuses have submitted proposals, according to the James G. Martin Center.

The three-year degree tests an old question in economics: how much of a degree’s value comes from what students learn, and how much from what it tells employers. Michael Spence shared the 2001 Nobel Prize in economics, in part, for showing how education can work as a signal, costly proof of ability that employers can’t see directly. A shorter degree is cheaper to get. Nobody knows yet whether it signals as much. Much of Europe has run three-year bachelor’s degrees since the Bologna reforms began in 1999.

RAND found that business, information technology, and cybersecurity look most workable for shorter degrees, while fields with clinical or licensing requirements are less adaptable. How graduate schools, employers, and licensing boards will treat the degrees remains uncertain, RAND found.

“We don’t have employers who said, ‘Gee, we’ve had three-year graduates,’” Madeleine Green, executive director of the College-in-3 Exchange, told Inside Higher Ed this month.

The programs are still small. Johnson & Wales University has 135 students across four three-year programs, and Weber State has 30 across three, according to Inside Higher Ed.

Faculty groups have pushed back. The American Association of University Professors and the American Federation of Teachers objected to Massachusetts’ approvals on grounds of “academic integrity.”

“This is not innovation. This is just cutting corners,” AAUP president Todd Wolfson told the Associated Press in August. A year less of tuition is also a year less of revenue for the schools.

Stitt’s other pitch to the CEOs was apprenticeships. He said he had visited Switzerland and Germany, where compulsory education stops after the first year of high school and students start working inside companies, and that Oklahoma made liability insurance fixes so companies could take on high school students. He told the room about a 19-year-old in Switzerland who wanted to be an architect. After three years at an architecture firm, she could work as a draftsperson, and university was still open to her.

“We’ve got to rethink that in our workforce needs here in the U.S.,” Stitt said.

Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news.

Bitcoin Miners Are Selling Their Stacks to Fund the AI Boom

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Bitcoin Miners Are Selling Their Stacks to Fund the AI Boom

Two of the biggest U.S. Bitcoin miners are turning their Bitcoin into AI infrastructure.

MARA sold 13,597 BTC after the end of September at an average price of about $81,039, raising roughly $1.1 billion, according to its third-quarter shareholder letter filed with the SEC.

That’s about 40% of the 34,012 BTC it held at the end of September. Combined with the 26,984 BTC it sold in the first nine months of the year, MARA has sold more than 40,000 BTC in 2026, worth roughly $2.5 billion at today’s prices.

MARA used $750 million of the proceeds to fully repay Bitcoin-backed loans it took out to fund its acquisition of the Long Ridge power plant.

The company said it intends to keep using Bitcoin to raise cash for AI infrastructure, convertible note puts that become exercisable next year and potential share buybacks. Its board has authorized a repurchase of up to $500 million.

MARA insisted the shift is “not a pivot away from bitcoin,” describing mining as its operating foundation while it builds out AI and high-performance computing infrastructure.

Meanwhile, Riot Platforms sold 20,264 BTC in the first nine months of the year for about $1.5 billion, primarily to fund data center development, and ended September with just 2,418 BTC, according to its Nov. 4 results.

The miner also signed a 15-year lease for its entire Corsicana, Texas, campus, covering 910 megawatts of capacity and worth about $28 billion over the initial term. CEO Jason Les called it “the largest lease in Riot’s history.” Riot’s quarterly filing identifies the tenant as Anthropic.

Mining economics help explain the shift. Riot’s Bitcoin mining revenue fell to $116 million in the third quarter from $160.8 million a year earlier, as the average value of each Bitcoin it mined dropped to $70,855 from $114,361.

Including equipment depreciation, Riot said it cost $78,805 to mine each Bitcoin, more than the coin’s average value over the quarter. MARA’s revenue fell 35% to $164.2 million.

The sales add supply to an already fragile market. Onchain analytics firm Glassnode said Tuesday that the two miners drove the recent selling by Bitcoin treasury companies, and that treasury firms outside those two were net buyers in November.

Strategy, the largest corporate holder, kept buying, adding 485 BTC last week to bring its holdings to 850,849 BTC, worth around $54 billion.

Ultraprocessed Foods Alter Food-Reward Brain Activity

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A controlled feeding trial reveals that just two weeks on a diet high in ultraprocessed foods alters brain reward responses in emerging adults ages 18 to 21, while leaving adults ages 22 to 25 unaffected. The fMRI study shows blunted activation in the developing orbitofrontal cortex, a key region for food valuation and self-control, even when diets are strictly matched for calories, sugar, fat, and nutrients.

Ultraprocessed foods, products formulated with industrial processing methods and ingredients rarely found in a home kitchen, account for more than half of the calories consumed by young adults in the United States. While extensive research links these foods to obesity, metabolic syndrome, and cardiovascular disease, neuroscientists are increasingly uncovering how industrial processing directly interacts with the developing human brain.

In a controlled feeding study published this month in the journal Obesity, researchers at Virginia Tech’s Fralin Biomedical Research Institute at VTC and Department of Human Nutrition, Foods, and Exercise found that emerging adults ages 18 to 21 exhibit distinct neural changes in food-reward circuitry after consuming an ultraprocessed diet for just two weeks.

Led by first author Dr. Emma Leslie alongside neuroscientist Dr. Alex DiFeliceantonio and registered dietitian Dr. Brenda Davy, the findings complement earlier results from the same trial showing that 18- to 21-year-olds consumed excess calories at a buffet meal after eating ultraprocessed foods, even when they were no longer hungry.

To separate the effects of industrial food processing from basic nutritional content, the researchers recruited 22 healthy adults ages 18 to 25 (including seven participants ages 21 or younger) for a randomized crossover feeding trial. Each participant followed two strictly controlled diets for two weeks apiece, separated by a four-week washout period:

Critically, both diets were engineered to have identical macronutrient profiles (50% carbohydrates, 35% fat, 15% protein), matching levels of fiber, added sugars, saturated fat, sodium, energy density, and overall nutritional quality, calibrated to keep each participant’s body weight stable.

Before and after each two-week dietary phase, participants underwent functional MRI (fMRI) brain scans while receiving small sips of an ultraprocessed milkshake or a tasteless control solution through a specialized delivery tube.

When the researchers analyzed the neuroimaging data, they discovered a striking developmental divide:

“Among the younger participants, consuming a diet high in ultraprocessed foods for just two weeks was associated with decreased brain response to a rewarding food — a milkshake — in the orbitofrontal cortex,” explained Leslie, who conducted the work as a doctoral student at Virginia Tech and is now a postdoctoral associate at the University of Michigan.

Located just behind the eyes, the orbitofrontal cortex is one of the final regions of the human brain to finish maturing, continuing to remodel into the mid- to late 20s. It plays a central role in evaluating food rewards, regulating cravings, and exerting executive self-control to stop eating.

Because the two diets were matched across nutritional metrics, the findings point toward non-nutritive properties of ultraprocessed foods, such as chemical additives, emulsifiers, texture alterations, or compounds generated during industrial processing, as potential drivers of altered brain signaling.

“This study is important because you have this change in an area of the brain known to govern self-control and potentially put a stop to eating,” said DiFeliceantonio, associate professor at the Fralin Biomedical Research Institute and interim co-director of its Center for Health Behaviors Research. “This is giving us insight into how ultraprocessed foods are affecting the bodies and brains of younger people, who might be especially vulnerable to their effects. And that’s important to know for things like dietary guidelines, doctor recommendations, and policy decisions.”

While the researchers note that the sample size was small and the age-based findings were exploratory, and did not directly correlate individual fMRI shifts with buffet calorie intake, the results highlight adolescence and early adulthood as critical windows of neurodevelopmental sensitivity.

Next, DiFeliceantonio and Davy plan to investigate whether adolescents under 18, who consume the highest proportion of ultraprocessed foods of any U.S. demographic, show even stronger neural sensitivity, and whether brain responses recover once individuals return to whole-food diets.

How do ultraprocessed foods affect the brains of young adults?

A Virginia Tech study found that after eating a diet high in ultraprocessed foods for just two weeks, young adults ages 18 to 21 showed decreased brain activity in the orbitofrontal cortex, a region involved in food reward, valuation, and self-control, when tasting a milkshake. Adults ages 22 to 25 did not show these brain changes.

Was the brain effect simply caused by extra sugar, fat, or calories?

No. Both the ultraprocessed diet and the unprocessed comparison diet contained the exact same amounts of calories, carbohydrates, fat, protein, added sugars, saturated fat, fiber, and sodium. This suggests that industrial food processing or additives themselves, rather than basic nutrients, drove the brain changes.

Why are 18- to 21-year-olds more sensitive to ultraprocessed foods than slightly older adults?

The orbitofrontal cortex is one of the last brain regions to finish developing, continuing to mature into the mid- to late 20s. Because this self-control and reward-evaluation circuit is still actively wiring in 18- to 21-year-olds, it appears more sensitive to dietary shifts.

Funding: The research was supported by a grant to DiFeliceantonio and Davy from the Eunice Kennedy Shriver National Institute of Child Health and Human Development, part of the National Institutes of Health. Leslie was also supported with an exercise health sciences graduate fellowship from Dr. Ray A. Gaskins.

The average American consumes 55% of daily energy from ultraprocessed foods (UPF), created through industrial processes and additives not used at home. We investigated if a high-UPF diet alters brain response to milkshake compared with a diet free from UPF in emerging adults, who are in a critical brain development period and typically consume high amounts of UPF.

In a randomized controlled crossover trial participants aged 18–25 years completed two 2-week controlled feeding periods including a UPF (81% UPF) and non-UPF (0% UPF) diet. Before and after each diet intervention, participants consumed milkshake concomitant with functional magnetic resonance imaging.

In the entire cohort, there were no differences between diet conditions in brain response. An exploratory analysis revealed orbitofrontal cortex response to milkshake decreased after the UPF diet and increased following the non-UPF diet in late adolescents (18–21 years) but not young adults (22–25 years). In all participants, habitual UPF intake (g) was positively associated with orbitofrontal cortex response to milkshake, independent of diet condition.

An acute UPF dietary intervention may only alter brain response in late adolescents. Further work is needed to determine potential vulnerability of late adolescents to changes in dietary UPF on brain response to rewards.

ClinicalTrials.gov identifier NCT05550818
